# But I think we can do even better.

This is the project I would actually submit.

# AI-Driven Website Intelligence Platform

Not

Website Scanner.

Not

Security Scanner.

Instead

## "AI Website Intelligence Platform"

The idea is:

A business enters its website URL.

The platform performs:
- Performance analysis (Core Web Vitals, Lighthouse metrics)
- Security assessment (OWASP-inspired checks, SSL, headers, cookies, outdated libraries)
- Accessibility evaluation
- SEO health
- Uptime and response-time monitoring
- Technology stack detection
- AI-generated explanation of findings
- AI-generated prioritized action plan
- Overall Trust Index (0–100)
- Performance Index (0–100)
- Security Index (0–100)
- Weekly trend analysis (if monitored over time)

### Why this stands out

The novelty is **decision support**, not just detection. Existing tools like Lighthouse, GTmetrix, or Mozilla Observatory each focus on one area. Your platform combines multiple assessments and uses AI to answer the questions businesses actually ask:

- Which issue should I fix first?
- Which issue hurts users the most?
- Which issue is the biggest security risk?
- If I only have two hours this week, what gives the highest impact?

This "AI prioritization layer" is the USP rather than another scanner.

### Why it fits your constraints

- **3–3.5 months:** Achievable by integrating existing scanners and building the AI analysis/dashboard yourself.
- **Research paper:** You can build or augment a dataset of website metrics and train models to predict trust/risk scores or issue severity. Public sources such as HTTP Archive, Core Web Vitals datasets, and security header datasets can support this.
- **Target audience:** Small businesses, startups, freelancers, educational institutions, agencies, and developers.
- **Future scope:** CI/CD integration, continuous monitoring, cloud deployment, browser extension, API version, enterprise dashboards.

---

## One important suggestion

I would **avoid** making the project just another "AI cybersecurity dashboard." Hundreds of colleges build log-analysis dashboards every year, and they often look similar.

Instead, position it as a **Website Intelligence Platform** that brings together security, performance, and AI-driven recommendations. That aligns naturally with your project title while giving you a clearer product, a broader audience, and a stronger research story.

I think this direction has the best balance of:
- genuine usefulness,
- market relevance,
- available datasets,
- publishable research potential,
- and a realistic development timeline for a beginner team.

From here, we can take it much further by designing the full product like a startup: defining the architecture, AI models, dataset strategy, research contribution, system modules, technology stack, and a week-by-week implementation roadmap. That level of planning will also make your PRD and review documents much stronger.

# One thing I would NOT do

Please don't make the mistake that **90% of engineering students make**.

They take existing tools like:

- Lighthouse
- OWASP ZAP
- Postman
- SSL Labs

Run them,

Display graphs,

Call it AI.

That will not get accepted in a good conference or journal.

The AI should make **new decisions** from the collected data.

Examples include:

- Predicting the overall risk score from multiple metrics.
- Ranking vulnerabilities by likely business impact rather than CVSS alone.
- Predicting performance degradation based on historical scans.
- Recommending the smallest set of fixes that yields the largest improvement in trust score.

Those are research contributions because you're creating an intelligence layer instead of another reporting dashboard.

## Prompt:

/strategy how can i identify these gaps and then turn them novelty and make research paper ?? what exactly will i write in my research paper?

## Response:
7/15/2026, 9:47:44 AM

This is **the most important question** in research, and honestly, most students misunderstand it.

They think:

> Read 6 papers → find one missing feature → add it → publish.

**That is NOT research.**

Research is about **identifying an unsolved problem**, proving that existing methods don't solve it well, and proposing a better solution.

Let's build this from scratch.

---

# Step 1: What is a research paper?

A research paper is **not** a project report.

A project report says:

> "We built a website scanner."

A research paper says:

> "Current website scanners have three major limitations. We propose a new AI-based framework that overcomes these limitations. Experimental evaluation shows improved prioritization and more useful recommendations."

See the difference?

The paper is about **the idea and the evidence**, not just the software.

---

# Step 2: Every research paper has this structure

```
Problem
      ↓
Existing Solutions
      ↓
Limitations (Research Gap)
      ↓
Our Proposed Method
      ↓
Experiments
      ↓
Results
      ↓
Conclusion
```

Everything revolves around the **gap**.

---

# Step 3: Where do research gaps come from?

A gap **does not mean**:

> "This feature doesn't exist."

It means something like:

- Existing solutions solve only part of the problem.
- Existing methods are inaccurate.
- Existing methods are slow.
- Existing methods don't work together.
- Existing methods don't explain results well.
- Existing methods are too expensive.
- Existing methods require expert knowledge.

These are all valid research gaps.

---

# Example using your project

Imagine you read this paper:

**Paper A**

It detects website vulnerabilities.

It outputs:

```
SQL Injection
XSS
CSRF

Security Score = 72
```

Now ask yourself:

### Question 1

Does it evaluate website performance?

No.

Gap #1

> Existing systems assess security but ignore performance.

---

### Question 2

Can it analyze APIs?

No.

Gap #2

> Existing systems are limited to websites.

---

### Question 3

Does it explain which issue should be fixed first?

No.

Gap #3

> Existing systems generate reports but do not prioritize fixes.

---

### Question 4

Does it predict future risks?

No.

Gap #4

> Existing systems provide only current-state assessment.

---

Notice something?

You didn't invent these gaps.

You **observed** them.

That's research.

---

# Step 4: Read all six papers like a detective

Don't ask:

> What did they build?

Ask:

> What did they NOT solve?

Make a table like this.

| Paper | Solves | Doesn't Solve |
|--------|---------|---------------|
| Paper 1 | Website security | No performance |
| Paper 2 | Website performance | No security |
| Paper 3 | API security | No website |
| Paper 4 | AI vulnerability detection | No explainability |
| Paper 5 | Scanner comparison | No prioritization |
| Paper 6 | HTTPS assessment | No API support |

Now look vertically.

```
Paper 1

No Performance

Paper 2

No Security

Paper 3

No Website

Paper 4

No Prioritization

Paper 5

No AI

Paper 6

No Unified Score
```

Patterns begin to appear.

Those patterns become your research gap.

---

# Step 5: Turn gaps into novelty

This is the magic step.

Gap:

```
Security only
```

Novelty:

```
Security + Performance
```

---

Gap:

```
Website only
```

Novelty:

```
Website + REST APIs
```

---

Gap:

```
Static report
```

Novelty:

```
AI-generated recommendations
```

---

Gap:

```
100 vulnerabilities listed
```

Novelty:

```
AI prioritizes the top 5 fixes based on business impact.
```

---

Gap:

```
Only current analysis
```

Novelty:

```
Trend prediction using ML.
```

---

# Step 6: Don't add random AI

This is where most engineering projects fail.

Bad novelty:

```
We used ChatGPT.
```

That is not research.

Good novelty:

```
We designed an AI model that combines 35 security metrics and 20 performance metrics to predict an overall Website Trust Score and recommend the highest-impact fixes.
```

Now that's research.

---

# Step 7: What exactly will your research paper contain?

Let's imagine your project is finalized.

Your paper might look like this.

---

## Title

**AI-Driven Unified Security and Performance Assessment Framework for Websites and REST APIs**

---

## Abstract

One-page summary.

Example:

> Existing assessment tools evaluate security and performance independently, forcing developers to use multiple platforms and manually prioritize issues. We propose an AI-driven unified framework that analyzes websites and REST APIs, computes integrated trust scores, predicts risk levels, and generates explainable recommendations. Experiments demonstrate improved decision support compared with traditional tools.

---

## Introduction

Discuss:

- Why cybersecurity matters.
- Growth of APIs.
- Importance of website performance.
- Why current tools are fragmented.

End with:

> Existing systems fail to provide unified, intelligent assessment.

---

## Literature Review

Summarize the six papers.

Example:

```
Paper 1

Security assessment

↓

Only security

↓

No AI

↓

No performance
```

Repeat for all papers.

---

## Research Gap

This is where you write something like:

> Although numerous tools assess website security and API vulnerabilities, most operate independently and provide isolated reports. Existing approaches rarely combine security and performance metrics into a unified assessment, lack AI-based prioritization of remediation actions, and provide limited support for business-oriented decision making.

This paragraph is the bridge between the literature review and your proposal.

---

## Proposed Methodology

This is **your contribution**.

Explain your architecture.

```
User

↓

Website/API

↓

Scanner Layer

↓

Security Features

↓

Performance Features

↓

AI Engine

↓

Trust Score

↓

Recommendation Engine

↓

Dashboard
```

This section explains how your system works conceptually.

---

## Dataset

Describe:

- Which public datasets you used.
- Which features you extracted.
- Any data you generated from your own scans.
- How you cleaned and prepared the data.

---

## AI Model

Examples:

- Random Forest
- XGBoost
- LightGBM
- Explainable AI (SHAP/LIME)
- Multi-criteria scoring engine

Explain **why** you chose it and how it maps features to outputs.

---

## Experiments

This is where you evaluate your approach.

For example:

Compare your AI prioritization against a baseline such as severity-only ranking.

Measure things like:

- Accuracy of risk prediction (if you have labeled data).
- Precision/Recall/F1 for classification tasks.
- Correlation between predicted trust score and known risk labels.
- User study: time saved in deciding what to fix.
- Improvement in recommendation quality.

---

## Results

Tables and graphs.

Example:

| Method | Average Time to Prioritize | Issues Correctly Prioritized |
|---------|---------------------------:|-----------------------------:|
| Traditional severity list | 18 min | 65% |
| Proposed AI model | 7 min | 89% |

Or:

| Model | Precision | Recall | F1-score |
|-------|----------:|--------:|---------:|
| Random Forest | ... | ... | ... |
| XGBoost | ... | ... | ... |

---

## Conclusion

Summarize:

- What problem you solved.
- Why your framework is better.
- Future work (continuous monitoring, cloud integration, browser extension, CI/CD integration).

---

# One thing I want you to remember

A research paper is **not judged by the size of the software**.

It is judged by **the strength of the research question**.

A weak question:

> "Can we make another website scanner?"

A strong question:

> "Can AI combine website and API security and performance metrics into a unified, explainable trust assessment that helps developers prioritize remediation more effectively?"

The second question naturally leads to literature review, research gaps, methodology, experiments, and publishable results.

---

## My suggestion before writing a single line of code

Create a **Research Matrix**. This will become the backbone of both your literature review and your paper.

| Paper | Objective | Dataset | Method | Results | Limitation | Research Gap | Our Solution |
|--------|-----------|---------|--------|---------|------------|--------------|--------------|

Fill this table for **15–20 papers** (not just 6). Once it's complete, the common limitations will almost reveal your novelty on their own. At that point, writing the literature review, defining the research gap, and even drafting the research paper becomes much more systematic instead of feeling like guesswork.

# But I think we can make the problem even stronger.

I don't think your project should be marketed as

> Website Scanner

Instead,

## AI Digital Health Checkup

Think of a human body.

Doctor checks

- Blood Pressure
- Sugar
- ECG
- Heart
- Kidney

Then says

> Overall Health = Good

Your project does exactly the same for digital assets.

Checks

- Security
- Performance
- API
- SSL
- Headers
- SEO
- Accessibility

Then says

> Your Digital Health is 76%.

That analogy is intuitive for non-technical users.

---

# Here's what I would avoid.

Don't say:

> "Our system scans websites."

That's a feature.

Instead say:

> "Our system helps organizations understand the overall health, security posture, and operational readiness of their digital assets through AI-assisted analysis and prioritized recommendations."

That's the value proposition.

---

# There is still one weakness.

I'll be critical here.

Your current idea is **good**, but not yet a publishable research contribution.

Why?

Because you are still **collecting outputs from existing tools**.

Reviewers may ask:

> "Where is the AI?"

If the AI only summarizes reports, that won't be enough.

---

# Here's how I would make it research-worthy.

Instead of saying

> AI summarizes the report.

Say

> We propose a **Unified Trust Assessment Model (UTAM)**.

Inputs:

- Security metrics
- Performance metrics
- API metrics
- HTTP headers
- SSL quality
- Core Web Vitals
- Historical trends

Outputs:

- Trust Score
- Risk Prediction
- Business Impact Score
- Prioritized Fix Ranking

The novelty isn't that you used Lighthouse or ZAP.

The novelty is **the model that fuses heterogeneous signals into a unified, explainable assessment**.

That is something you design, evaluate, and defend in a research paper.

---

## My conclusion

I **would recommend this direction**, but with one important refinement:

Don't build **another scanner**.

Build an **AI decision-support platform**.

Your competitors (Lighthouse, GTMetrix, OWASP ZAP, SSL Labs, Mozilla Observatory, Postman) are excellent at measuring individual aspects. Your platform should answer the question they don't:

> **"Given everything we know about this website or API, what should this organization do next, and why?"**

If you can answer that convincingly—with a clear methodology and experimental validation—you'll have a project that is useful, technically interesting, and has a much stronger chance of leading to a solid research paper.

This is the architecture I'd recommend:

```
User

↓

Website/API URL

↓

Scanner Layer
   • Lighthouse
   • Security Headers
   • SSL Checker
   • Tech Stack Detection
   • API Tester

↓

↓

Random Forest / XGBoost
   → Predict Trust Score

↓

SHAP (Explainability)
   → Identify the metrics driving the score

↓

LLM Layer
   → Convert technical findings into plain-English recommendations

↓

   → Security Score
   → Performance Score
   → Trust Score
   → Risk Level
   → Top 5 Fixes

↓

## However, there is one **major challenge** we haven't solved yet—and it's the most important one.

> **Where do the labels (ground truth) for "Trust Score" come from?**

Without reliable labels, you can't train a supervised ML model properly. We need a defensible way to generate or obtain those labels; otherwise, reviewers will immediately question the validity of the model.

**I think this is the next problem we should solve before we write even one line of code.**

Once we solve the labeling strategy, everything else—dataset creation, ML training, evaluation metrics, and the research paper—falls into place naturally. In my opinion, that single decision will determine whether your project looks like a typical engineering dashboard or a genuine AI research project.

I think we should stop discussing architecture for a moment.

**The next step is NOT coding.**
**The next step is NOT choosing React or Python.**
**The next step is NOT writing the paper.**

The next step is **proving that your research idea deserves to exist.**

This is how I would do it if I were doing an IEEE/Springer paper.

---

# Phase 1 (Current Phase) - Research Validation ✅

**Goal:** Answer one question:

> **"Is our idea actually novel and publishable?"**

### Deliverables

- ✅ Final problem statement
- ✅ Target users
- ✅ Existing solutions
- ✅ Research gaps
- ✅ Novelty
- ✅ Final title

**Do NOT write a single line of code yet.**

---

# Phase 2 - Literature Survey

This is where we'll spend most of our effort.

Don't read 5 papers.

Read **20–30 quality papers**.

Create a spreadsheet like this:

| Paper | Year | Problem | Method | Dataset | Limitation | Gap |
|--------|------|---------|--------|---------|------------|-----|
| Paper 1 | 2025 | Website Security | CNN | CICIDS | No performance | Security only |
| Paper 2 | 2024 | API Security | Rule-based | OWASP | No AI | No prioritization |
| Paper 3 | 2026 | Website Audit | Hybrid | HTTP Archive | No explainability | No business impact |

After 20–30 papers, patterns will emerge naturally.

---

# Phase 3 - Define the Research Contribution

This is the **most important deliverable**.

Write exactly one sentence.

Example:

> *We propose an AI-driven unified framework that combines website and REST API security metrics with performance metrics to predict an explainable trust score and prioritize remediation actions.*

Everything else in the paper revolves around this sentence.

---

# Phase 4 - Dataset Strategy

This is where we answer:

- Which public datasets will we use?
- What additional data will we generate ourselves?
- What is the prediction target?
- Which features are available?
- Do we have labels or do we create them?

Only after answering these should we decide whether we use:
- Random Forest
- XGBoost
- Rule-based scoring
- SHAP
- LLM

---

# Phase 5 - Research Paper Draft

Now write:

- Abstract
- Introduction
- Literature Review
- Gap Analysis
- Proposed Methodology
- Expected Contributions

At this stage, it's okay if experiments are still pending.

---

# Phase 6 - Build the Product

Only now start coding.

---

## Response:
7/15/2026, 11:31:49 AM

**Short answer: NO.**

There is **no widely accepted academic framework** that combines **all** of the following into one AI-driven system:

- ✅ Website Security
- ✅ Website Performance
- ✅ REST API Security
- ✅ REST API Performance
- ✅ AI-based decision support/prioritization
- ✅ Explainable unified assessment

However...

There are **commercial products** that combine **parts** of this idea.

Examples:
- **Qualys TotalAppSec** - unified web & API security with AI-assisted risk prioritization. ([docs.qualys.com](https://docs.qualys.com/en/tas/latest/get_started/totalappsec.htm?utm_source=chatgpt.com))
- **Google Cloud WAAP** - unified protection for web applications and APIs. ([cloud.google.com](https://cloud.google.com/security/solutions/web-app-and-api-protection?utm_source=chatgpt.com))
- **Microsoft Defender for Cloud** - API security posture management with prioritized recommendations. ([learn.microsoft.com](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/defender-for-cloud/api-security-posture-overview?utm_source=chatgpt.com))
- **Google Apigee** - API security scoring and monitoring over time. ([docs.cloud.google.com](https://docs.cloud.google.com/apigee/docs/api-security/security-scores?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### So what does this mean?

❌ **Your current idea ("combine website + API + AI + dashboard") is NOT novel enough** as a research contribution.

Commercial vendors already provide unified platforms.

---

### The novelty has to be in the **AI method**, not the dashboard.

For example:

- A **new explainable Digital Trust Index** that combines security and performance metrics.
- A **new AI prioritization algorithm** that ranks fixes based on business impact instead of severity.
- A **new ML model** that predicts future degradation in website/API health.
- A **new multi-factor trust assessment framework** validated experimentally.

These are publishable research contributions.

### My conclusion

I would **not** publish a paper saying:

> "We built an AI dashboard for website and API assessment."

I **would** publish a paper saying:

> "We propose a novel AI-based Digital Trust Assessment Framework (or prioritization model) and implement it in a unified website/API assessment platform."

The **framework/model** is the research.
The **dashboard** is the implementation.

---

**Sources:**

- [TotalAppSec - Unified Application Risk Management](https://docs.qualys.com/en/tas/latest/get_started/totalappsec.htm?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Web App and API Protection (WAAP) | Google Cloud](https://cloud.google.com/security/solutions/web-app-and-api-protection?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [API security posture overview - Microsoft Defender for Cloud | Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/defender-for-cloud/api-security-posture-overview?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Risk assessment overview and UI  |  Apigee  |  Google Cloud Documentation](https://docs.cloud.google.com/apigee/docs/api-security/security-scores?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

# Final Research Problem

> **Current website and API assessment tools generate large volumes of security and performance findings but lack intelligent, context-aware decision support that helps developers prioritize remediation based on security risk, performance impact, and business relevance.**

I think this is the strongest problem statement for your capstone.

---

# What is actually broken today?

These are the **real gaps** repeatedly discussed in recent literature.

### 1. Alert Overload ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Current tools detect hundreds of issues but don't tell developers what to fix first.

---

### 2. Static Risk Scoring ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Most rely on CVSS/OWASP severity.
Severity ≠ Actual business risk.

---

### 3. No Business Context ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Tools don't consider:

- Is this website an e-commerce site?
- Is this API public?
- Is login critical?

Same vulnerability ≠ same business impact.

---

### 4. Website & API Are Evaluated Separately ⭐⭐⭐⭐☆
Organizations use multiple disconnected tools.

No unified decision.

---

### 5. Poor Explainability ⭐⭐⭐⭐☆
AI models detect problems but don't explain **why** something should be prioritized.

---

### 6. No Decision Support ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Current tools provide reports.

They don't answer

> "What should I fix first?"

---

### 7. No Multi-factor Prioritization ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐

Existing tools mostly rank using

- CVSS
- OWASP Severity

They rarely combine

- exploitability
- business impact
- performance degradation
- user experience
- API criticality

into one priority.

---

### 8. Little Personalization ⭐⭐⭐⭐☆
Two companies receive identical reports although their priorities differ.

---

Existing approaches often emphasize detection and scoring rather than actionable decision support and integrated prioritization.

That conclusion is **defensible**, aligned with current literature, and provides a solid foundation for your research gap without making exaggerated claims. It also gives you room to define your own contribution in the next stage.

# My recommendation

If I were your supervisor, **I would not position your project as "another website scanner."**

I would position it as:

> **An AI-powered Decision Support System for Website and API Security & Performance Assessment.**

Notice the difference.

The scanner (Lighthouse, ZAP, HAR analysis, etc.) becomes the **data source**.

The **research contribution** is the AI layer that:
- integrates security + performance findings,
- prioritizes remediation using multiple factors,
- explains *why* an issue is important,
- and estimates its likely impact.

This aligns directly with the recurring gaps you've identified:
- lack of intelligent prioritization,
- fragmented security/performance evaluation,
- limited context-awareness,
- and insufficient explainability. It also avoids competing head-on with mature scanners, which already exist and are well validated. `computers-14-00446-with-cover.pdf` `RP1.pdf` `TSP_CMC_67536.pdf`

---

# One thing I would change

After reading your six papers, **I would slightly refine the research problem**.

Instead of:

> "The absence of intelligent decision support in existing website and API security/performance assessment tools..."

I would write:

> **Existing website and API assessment tools effectively detect security and performance issues but provide limited context-aware, explainable, and actionable decision support for remediation prioritization. Consequently, developers must manually interpret fragmented reports, increasing remediation effort and delaying effective risk mitigation.**

I think this is a stronger, more defensible research problem because it **doesn't claim current tools are ineffective**-it acknowledges what they do well and clearly states what remains unsolved. That is exactly the kind of framing reviewers and faculty tend to accept.

# ⭐ The title I would submit

## **AI-Driven Intelligent Decision Support System for Website and API Security & Performance Assessment**

### Research Problem
Current website and API assessment tools generate fragmented security and performance reports but provide limited **context-aware, explainable, and actionable decision support**, forcing developers to manually prioritize remediation.

### Research Gap
Existing solutions excel at **detecting** issues but provide limited support for **intelligently prioritizing remediation by combining security risk, performance impact, and contextual information**.

### Novelty
Develop an **AI Decision Support Engine** that:
- Integrates website security, API security, and performance findings.
- Produces a **prioritized remediation roadmap** instead of just raw reports.
- Explains **why** each recommendation is important (Explainable AI).
- Estimates the expected impact of fixing each issue (security, performance, user experience).
- Provides a unified **Digital Trust Score** derived from multiple assessment dimensions rather than relying on a single scanner's score.

---

## One suggestion

I would **avoid** making **"Digital Trust Score"** part of the title.

Instead, make it your **USP (Unique Selling Proposition)**.

This is **THE MOST IMPORTANT DESIGN DECISION** of the project.

Because if we misuse the LLM, your faculty will immediately ask:

> **"Why can't I just paste the report into ChatGPT?"**

If we use the LLM correctly, that question disappears.

---

# First, understand this.

There are **3 possible ways** to use AI.

## ❌ Wrong Way 1

```text
Website URL

↓

ChatGPT

↓

Analysis
```

No.

ChatGPT doesn't scan websites like Lighthouse or OWASP ZAP.

---

## ❌ Wrong Way 2

```text
Website URL

↓

LLM

↓

Priority
```

Also no.

LLMs are not reliable for numerical risk scoring or prioritization.

---

## ✅ Correct Way

```text
Website URL

↓

Assessment Tools

↓

Structured Data

↓

ML Model

↓

Priority Scores

↓

LLM

↓

Natural Language Report
```

Notice something.

The LLM is the **last step**, not the first.

---

# What exactly does the LLM receive?

Not the website.

It receives a JSON like this.

```json
{
  "website": "abc.com",
  "security_score": 71,
  "performance_score": 64,
  "top_findings": [
    {
      "issue": "Missing CSP",
      "priority": "Critical",
      "confidence": 0.94,
      "expected_security_gain": 18
    },
    {
      "issue": "Images not compressed",
      "priority": "High",
      "expected_performance_gain": 14
    }
  ]
}
```

This JSON is created by **your backend**, not by the LLM.

---

# Then the LLM does this.

Instead of showing

```
Issue:
Missing CSP

Priority:
Critical
```

LLM writes

> Your website does not define a **Content Security Policy (CSP)**. This makes it more vulnerable to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) attacks. Since your login page handles user credentials, this issue should be addressed immediately. Enabling CSP is expected to improve your overall security posture significantly.

That's a huge improvement for users.

---

# LLM Job 1 — Explain

Technical

↓

Simple English

Example

```
HSTS Missing
```

↓

LLM

↓

> HSTS forces browsers to use secure HTTPS connections, preventing downgrade attacks. Enabling it improves user safety during communication.

---

# LLM Job 2 — Summarize

Instead of

```
35 pages report
```

User gets

> Your website is generally secure but suffers from three high-impact issues that affect both security and loading speed.

---

# LLM Job 3 — Action Plan

Instead of

```
120 findings
```

LLM creates

```
Week 1
✔ Enable CSP
✔ Enable HSTS

Week 2
✔ Compress Images
✔ Browser Caching

Week 3
✔ API Rate Limiting
```

Developers love this.

---

# LLM Job 4 — PDF Report

Instead of generating a boring report

LLM writes

```
Executive Summary

Overall Health

Top Risks

Recommendations

Expected Improvements

Conclusion
```

Automatically.

---

# LLM Job 5 — Chat Assistant (Optional)

After scanning

User asks

```
Why is CSP important?
```

LLM answers.

Or

```
How do I fix this in React?
```

LLM explains.

This is a nice extra feature.

---

# What should the LLM NEVER do?

Never let it

❌ Predict risk

❌ Calculate priority

❌ Give scores

❌ Decide severity

Those should come from your ML model or deterministic rules.

---

# Where will the LLM get its knowledge?

Not from memory alone.

We'll use **Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)**.

Knowledge Base

- OWASP Top 10
- OWASP API Security Top 10
- Mozilla Web Security Guidelines
- MDN documentation
- NIST guidance

When the model explains "Missing CSP," it retrieves the relevant guidance and generates a grounded explanation instead of inventing one.

This reduces hallucinations and is much easier to defend academically.

---

# Which LLM should we use?

For a student project:

- **Gemini API** ✅
- **OpenAI GPT API** ✅
- **Llama 3 / Qwen (local, optional)**

I would recommend **Gemini API** because it has a generous free tier for development and is easy to integrate.

---

# Your complete architecture

```text
                USER
                  │
                  ▼
        Enter Website/API URL
                  │
                  ▼
         Assessment Engine
   (Lighthouse + ZAP + SSL + Headers)
                  │
                  ▼
        Feature Extraction (KPIs)
                  │
                  ▼
      ML / Rule-Based Decision Engine
      ├── Priority Score
      ├── Security Score
      ├── Performance Score
      └── Digital Trust Score
                  │
                  ▼
            LLM + RAG Layer
      ├── Explain findings
      ├── Generate remediation roadmap
      ├── Write executive summary
      ├── Answer user questions
      └── Create PDF report
                  │
                  ▼
         Dashboard + Download Report
```

---

## Here's one improvement I'd make to our plan

After everything we've discussed, I would **change the name "AI Decision Engine"** into **two separate modules**:

### 1. Intelligence Engine (Your Research)
This contains:
- ML model (trained on public datasets like NVD/EPSS/CISA)
- Rule-based reasoning
- Priority scoring
- Digital Trust Score

This is what your **research paper** is about.

### 2. AI Assistant (LLM)
This contains:
- Natural language explanations
- Executive summaries
- Step-by-step remediation guidance
- Interactive Q&A
- Report generation

This is what users interact with.

This separation makes your project much easier to explain in the viva:

> **"The Intelligence Engine makes the decisions. The LLM communicates those decisions clearly."**

That answer is technically sound, easy to defend, and avoids the impression that you're relying on an LLM to "do all the AI."

# But here's the biggest problem...

I think we're missing something.

### Where does the label "Priority" come from?

Because NVD gives **CVSS**.

EPSS gives **Probability**.

CISA gives **Exploited/Not**.

**None of them gives "Priority".**

So we still need to define the target variable.

---

# This is where I would make ONE change.

Instead of training the model to predict **Priority** (which doesn't exist), I would train it to predict:

> **Whether a vulnerability is likely to be in the CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog.**

Why?

Because:
- **Features** come from NVD (CVSS, attack vector, privileges, etc.).
- **Label** comes from CISA KEV (`Yes` / `No`).
- EPSS becomes an additional feature or a benchmark.
- This is a **real supervised learning problem** with publicly available labels.

Then, inside your application:

- ML predicts **exploitation likelihood**.
- You combine that prediction with:
  - Website findings,
  - Performance metrics,
  - Business rules,
- to generate your **Adaptive Remediation Priority**.

# 🥈 Dataset 2 - EPSS (Exploit Prediction Scoring System)

Maintained by **FIRST.org**.

**Purpose**

# 🥉 Dataset 3 - CISA KEV (Known Exploited Vulnerabilities)

Maintained by **CISA (US Government)**.

**Purpose**

Provides the **ground-truth label**:
- Has this vulnerability actually been exploited in the wild?

This is extremely useful for supervised learning.

**Contains**

- CVE ID
- Vendor
- Product
- Vulnerability Name
- Date Added
- Due Date
- Required Action

Excellent. These are exactly the kinds of questions a Review-I panel asks.

If I were on your evaluation panel, these are the questions I would ask, and these are the answers I would expect from a strong team.

---

# 1. Why will people trust your platform?

This is probably the most important question.

## Wrong Answer ❌

> Because we use AI.

---

## Correct Answer ✅

> Our platform does **not blindly rely on AI**. It combines **industry-standard assessment tools** (such as Lighthouse, OWASP ZAP, Security Headers, SSL analysis) with **public cybersecurity intelligence** (NVD, EPSS, CISA KEV). The AI does not invent vulnerabilities—it only prioritizes and explains verified findings.

Further,

- We display the **source** of every finding.
- We provide a **confidence score**.
- Every recommendation is **explainable**.
- Users can verify recommendations against official references like OWASP and NIST.

**Key sentence:**

> "The AI assists decision-making; it does not replace established security scanners."

---

# 2. Technical Feasibility

### Question

> Is this technically possible?

### Answer

Yes.

We are **not building vulnerability scanners from scratch**.

Instead, we integrate existing, well-established tools:

- Lighthouse → Performance
- OWASP ZAP → Security
- Security Headers → HTTP security
- SSL/TLS Analysis
- Gemini API → Report generation
- Public datasets (NVD, EPSS, CISA KEV) → ML training

The main technical contribution is the **AI Decision Support Engine**, which combines these outputs into prioritized recommendations.

---

# 3. Resource Feasibility

### Question

> Do you have the required resources?

### Answer

Yes.

Everything required is publicly available.

| Resource | Availability |
|----------|-------------|
| NVD | Free |
| EPSS | Free |
| CISA KEV | Free |
| Lighthouse | Open Source |
| OWASP ZAP | Open Source |
| Python | Free |
| FastAPI | Free |
| React | Free |
| PostgreSQL | Free |
| GitHub | Free |
| Gemini API | Free tier available |

No specialized hardware is required.

---

# 4. Time Feasibility

### Question

> Can it be completed in one semester?

### Answer

Yes.

We divided the project into six phases:

- Research & Dataset
- Scanner Integration
- Dashboard Development
- ML Model
- LLM Integration
- Testing & Deployment

Each team member is responsible for one major module.

We are building on existing tools rather than developing scanners ourselves, making the timeline realistic.

---

# 5. Constraints Considered

## Budget

Almost zero.

Only optional deployment costs.

Everything else is open source.

---

## Time

12 weeks.

Modules developed in parallel.

---

## Skills

We are beginners.

Therefore we deliberately selected

- React
- FastAPI
- Scikit-learn
- Gemini API

instead of complex deep-learning architectures.

---

## Infrastructure

Normal laptop

Internet

GitHub

Python

No GPU required.

---

# 6. Risk Assessment

## Risk 1

Scanner integration fails.

### Mitigation

Use modular architecture.

If one scanner fails,

others continue.

---

## Risk 2

Dataset mismatch.

### Mitigation

All datasets use

CVE ID

which simplifies merging.

---

## Risk 3

LLM hallucination.

### Mitigation

LLM never detects vulnerabilities.

It only explains verified findings.

---

## Risk 4

Model accuracy is low.

### Mitigation

Compare multiple algorithms

(Random Forest, XGBoost, Logistic Regression)

and select the best-performing one.

---

# 7. Target Audience

Don't say

> Everyone.

That is a weak answer.

---

### Primary Target Audience

Small and Medium Enterprises (SMEs)

Reason

Most SMEs

cannot afford

enterprise solutions

like

- Qualys
- Tenable
- Rapid7

---

### Secondary Audience

- Freelance Web Developers
- Startup Teams
- DevOps Engineers
- QA Engineers
- Students learning secure development

---

### Tertiary Audience

Educational institutions

for website auditing.

---

# 8. What problem are you solving?

Answer

Current tools provide

large technical reports

but do not provide

intelligent,

context-aware,

prioritized remediation guidance.

Developers still spend considerable time deciding what to fix first.

Our platform reduces that decision effort.

---

# 9. Why can't developers just use ChatGPT?

Excellent faculty question.

Answer

ChatGPT does not perform website security scanning.

It lacks direct access to

- Lighthouse metrics
- OWASP ZAP results
- SSL analysis
- Security Headers

It also cannot reliably prioritize vulnerabilities based on structured cybersecurity datasets.

Our platform first collects verified technical findings using specialized tools and then uses AI only for prioritization and explanation.

---

# 10. Why can't users directly upload a Lighthouse report to ChatGPT?

Answer

They can obtain general suggestions.

However,

ChatGPT

- doesn't merge security findings,
- doesn't use NVD/EPSS/CISA knowledge,
- doesn't rank vulnerabilities using a trained ML model,
- doesn't produce a unified remediation roadmap.

Our platform combines

performance,

security,

API assessment,

and vulnerability intelligence

into one decision-support system.

---

# 11. What is your novelty?

Never say

> AI.

Instead

> Our novelty is an AI-assisted Decision Support Framework that integrates verified security and performance findings with public vulnerability intelligence (NVD, EPSS, and CISA KEV) to generate prioritized, explainable remediation recommendations rather than merely reporting issues.

---

# 12. Why is ML needed?

Answer

The ML model helps estimate which vulnerabilities deserve higher remediation priority based on historical vulnerability characteristics.

Instead of relying only on CVSS,

the model also considers

- exploit likelihood,
- vulnerability characteristics,
- and known exploitation history.

---

# 13. Why these datasets?

Answer

NVD provides detailed vulnerability characteristics.

EPSS provides exploitation probability.

CISA KEV provides real-world exploitation evidence.

Together they provide complementary information suitable for supervised machine learning.

---

# 14. What if your AI gives a wrong recommendation?

Answer

The platform is a

Decision Support System,

not an autonomous decision-maker.

The final decision remains with the developer.

Every recommendation includes

- confidence score,
- explanation,
- and supporting evidence.

---

# 15. Why should anyone use your product instead of existing scanners?

This is the question that decides your marks.

Answer

Existing tools specialize in **detection**.

Our platform focuses on **decision support**.

Instead of producing multiple disconnected reports, we provide a unified, prioritized remediation roadmap with explanations and expected impact.

We complement existing scanners rather than replacing them.

---

# 16. If Qualys, Rapid7, or Tenable already exist, why build this?

Answer

Those platforms are powerful but are primarily designed for enterprise environments and often require paid subscriptions, specialized knowledge, and significant setup.

Our goal is different:
- affordable and accessible for SMEs and students,
- combines website performance with security,
- emphasizes explainable recommendations,
- provides a simplified remediation roadmap.

---

# Questions I would definitely ask if I were your examiner

1. Why did you choose **FastAPI** over Django?
2. Why did you select **Random Forest/XGBoost** instead of a deep neural network?
3. Why is **CISA KEV** used as the ground truth?
4. How do you handle websites that don't expose an API?
5. How will you evaluate your ML model? (Accuracy, Precision, Recall, F1, ROC-AUC)
6. How will you validate that your recommendations are actually useful?
7. How will you ensure you don't overwhelm users with false positives?
8. What happens if Lighthouse and the security scanner give conflicting priorities?
9. How is your **Digital Trust Score** calculated?
10. Which parts of your system are deterministic (rule-based) and which use AI?

---

## My advice

Before coding anything, I would prepare a **50–60 question viva handbook** covering architecture, datasets, ML, frontend, backend, research gap, novelty, feasibility, ethics, and limitations.

I have seen many excellent projects lose marks not because of poor implementation, but because the team couldn't confidently answer these kinds of questions. A well-prepared Q&A document will give you a significant advantage during reviews and the final viva.

Perfect. Now we should stop thinking like developers and start thinking like **researchers**.

Since this is **Review-I**, your literature review article is **not your final research paper**. Its purpose is to convince your guide and panel that:

1. You understand the research domain.
2. You have studied existing work.
3. You have identified a genuine research gap.
4. Your proposed work addresses that gap.

---

# Which format should we follow?

Since your college hasn't specified one, **follow the IEEE conference paper format**.

This is the safest option because:
- Most engineering colleges accept it.
- It is the format you'll likely use for publication later.
- It already has professional margins, fonts, and layout.

---

# Page Formatting (IEEE Standard)

| Item | Standard |
|-------|----------|
| Paper Size | A4 |
| Columns | Two-column |
| Font | Times New Roman |
| Font Size | 10 pt (main text) |
| Title | 24 pt Bold |
| Author Names | 11 pt |
| Section Heading | 10 pt Bold |
| Line Spacing | Single |
| Margins | IEEE Template (don't change) |
| References | IEEE citation style [1], [2], [3] |

**Don't create your own formatting.** Download the official IEEE conference template and write inside it.

---

# Recommended Length

For Review-I:

**8–10 pages** (excluding references)

This is sufficient.

---

# Structure of the Literature Review Article

## 1. Title

Example:

> **A Literature Review on AI-Driven Intelligent Decision Support Systems for Website and API Security & Performance Assessment**

Notice:

Don't use your final project title.

Make it clear that this is a **literature review**.

---

## 2. Abstract (150–250 words)

Explain:

- Research area
- Importance
- Existing approaches
- Problems
- Purpose of this review

Do **not** discuss your project here.

---

## 3. Keywords (5–7)

Example:

- Website Security
- API Security
- Vulnerability Prioritization
- Artificial Intelligence
- Explainable AI
- Web Performance
- Decision Support

---

## 4. Introduction (1–1.5 pages)

Cover:

### Background
Why websites and APIs matter today.

### Problem
Why current assessment tools create challenges.

### Importance
Why intelligent prioritization is needed.

### Objective of Review
What this literature review aims to analyze.

---

## 5. Research Methodology (Very Important)

Almost every good review paper includes this.

Explain:

- Which databases you searched:
  - IEEE Xplore
  - Springer
  - ScienceDirect
  - ACM
  - MDPI
  - arXiv (if used)
- Search keywords
- Inclusion criteria
- Exclusion criteria

Example:

> Papers published between 2023–2026 focusing on website security, API security, vulnerability prioritization, AI, explainable AI, and website performance were considered.

This makes your review systematic.

---

# 6. Literature Review (Main Section)

Don't summarize paper by paper.

Instead, divide into themes.

## Theme 1: Website Security Assessment

Discuss:
- Existing scanners
- Vulnerability detection
- Limitations

Mention relevant papers.

End with a mini conclusion.

---

## Theme 2: REST API Security

Discuss:
- API vulnerabilities
- Detection approaches
- Current limitations

# Writing Style

Never write:

> I think...

> We think...

Instead:

> Existing studies indicate...

> Previous research demonstrates...

> The reviewed literature suggests...

Keep it objective and evidence-based.

---

# Suggested Page Distribution

| Section | Pages |
|----------|------:|
| Abstract | 0.5 |
| Introduction | 1 |
| Methodology | 0.5 |
| Literature Review | 3–4 |
| Comparative Analysis | 1 |
| Research Gaps | 1 |
| Proposed Direction | 0.5 |
| Conclusion | 0.5 |
| References | 1–2 |

Total: **8–10 pages**

---

# My recommendation

Based on everything we've done over the last few days, I **would not** write this as a simple "review article."

I would write it as a **Mini Systematic Literature Review (SLR)**.

That means:
- a clear search methodology,
- thematic organization instead of paper-by-paper summaries,
- comparison tables,
- evidence-backed research gaps,
- and a proposed future research direction.

This structure looks significantly more professional, is closer to publishable quality, and will make a much stronger impression during Review-I than a traditional literature summary.

## Prompt:
7/16/2026, 1:48:37 PM

IEEE format is for research paper right but this is review article so still??

## Response:
7/16/2026, 1:48:41 PM

**Excellent question.**

And the answer is **No, not necessarily.**

This is where many students get confused.

---

# Difference between the three

| Type | Purpose | Format |
|------|---------|---------|
| Research Paper | Present your own novel work | IEEE / Springer / Elsevier |
| Review Article | Summarize and analyze existing literature | Journal-specific (not fixed) |
| Literature Review (Review-I) | Academic report before project | Usually college-defined or free format |

---

## In your case

You are **not submitting a journal review article.**

You are submitting a **Literature Review Article to your faculty** for Review-I.

That means there is **no universal format** unless your college specifies one.

---

# So should you use IEEE?

### If your college has NOT given any template

Then **YES**, I still recommend using the **IEEE conference template** because:

- It looks professional.
- Faculty are familiar with it.
- Later, when you write your research paper, you can reuse much of the content.
- It avoids formatting issues.

But **the content is different**.

---

## IEEE Layout ≠ IEEE Research Paper

You can use the IEEE template while writing a **review article**.

The only difference is the sections.

Instead of

```
Methodology
Experiment
Results
```

you write

```
Introduction
Review Methodology
Literature Review
Comparative Analysis
Research Gaps
Proposed Research Direction
Conclusion
```

So you're using **IEEE formatting**, not an IEEE research-paper structure.

---

# If I were your supervisor

I would actually **NOT** use the IEEE two-column format for Review-I.

Instead, I would use a **journal-style single-column review article** because it is much easier to read and annotate.

### My recommended formatting

| Item | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Paper Size | A4 |
| Font | Times New Roman |
| Title | 18 pt Bold |
| Heading | 14 pt Bold |
| Subheading | 12 pt Bold |
| Body | 12 pt |
| Line Spacing | 1.5 |
| Margins | 1 inch on all sides |
| Alignment | Justified |
| References | IEEE style ([1], [2], [3]) |

This looks like a professional academic review report and is much easier for faculty to review than a dense two-column paper.

---

# What I would do if I were you

Since your faculty only said:

> **"Submit a Literature Review Article"**

and did **not** specify:

- IEEE
- Springer
- Elsevier
- APA
- MLA

I would prepare it as a **professional review report**, **not** as a publication-ready IEEE paper.

---

# My recommendation (Final)

For **Review-I**, use:

- ✅ Single-column layout
- ✅ Times New Roman
- ✅ 12 pt body
- ✅ 1.5 line spacing
- ✅ IEEE citation style for references
- ✅ 8–10 pages
- ✅ Professional headings
- ✅ Tables and figures

Then, when you later write the **actual research paper for publication**, convert the content into the required journal or conference template (IEEE, Springer, Elsevier, etc.).

---

## I have one suggestion that I think is even better

Before deciding the format, **ask your guide one simple question**:

> **"Sir/Ma'am, should the literature review article follow any specific template (IEEE/college format), or may we use a standard academic review format?"**

If they say **"No specific format"**, then I recommend the **single-column academic review format** above.

It is more readable, looks professional, and is the format I would personally choose for a Review-I submission.

# 18. Can this be completed in 3 months?

**Yes, with one simplification.**

I would **remove one assumption** from our earlier discussions:

> ❌ "Map every scanner finding directly to an exact CVE."

That is difficult and often impossible because many scanner findings (e.g., missing CSP, missing HSTS, poor caching) are **configuration weaknesses**, not specific CVEs.

Instead, I recommend this implementation:

- **Security scanner findings** (e.g., Missing CSP, Weak TLS, SQL Injection) become **security features**.
- **Performance metrics** (LCP, CLS, TTFB, etc.) become **performance features**.
- The **NVD + EPSS + CISA KEV datasets** are used to train a **risk model** and to enrich recommendations with real-world vulnerability intelligence, rather than trying to map every website issue to a unique CVE.

This architecture is:
- **Technically feasible** for a 3-month student project.
- **Consistent** with your research goal of intelligent decision support.
- **Much easier to defend** in front of your faculty because you're not claiming a capability (perfect CVE mapping) that is unrealistic.

I would freeze this architecture and build the project around it. It is ambitious enough for a capstone, yet realistic enough that three beginners can complete it successfully.

i have a doubt
earlier we discussed that the user will get top issues along with impact on the security and performance
like if this improved security +10 % performance 12%

## Response:
7/16/2026, 7:37:42 PM

Excellent catch.

**This is exactly the kind of question your faculty will ask.**

And honestly, **our previous idea had a flaw**.

---

# The problem

We said

```
Fix CSP

↓

Security +18%

Performance +0%
```

or

```
Compress Images

↓

Performance +15%
```

The question is:

> **How do we know it will improve by exactly 15% or 18%?**

We **don't**.

There is **no public dataset** that says:

```
Missing CSP

↓

+18% Security
```

That number would be **made up**, and in a viva, the panel could ask:

> "How did you calculate +18%?"

We would not have a defensible answer.

---

# So I would change this.

Instead of showing

```
Security +18%
```

show

```
Expected Impact: HIGH
```

or

```
Expected Security Impact

★★★★★

Expected Performance Impact

★★☆☆☆
```

---

# Example

Instead of

```
Enable CSP

Security +18%
```

show

```
Enable CSP

Priority : Critical

Expected Security Impact : High

Performance Impact : None

Estimated Fix Complexity : Easy

Confidence : 94%
```

This is realistic.

---

# Another example

Instead of

```
Compress Images

Performance +12%
```

show

```
Compress Images

Expected Performance Impact : High

Security Impact : None

Estimated Implementation Time : 15–20 minutes

Confidence : High
```

---

# Even better

We can classify impact into categories.

| Level | Meaning |
|--------|---------|
| Very High | ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| High | ⭐⭐⭐⭐ |
| Medium | ⭐⭐⭐ |
| Low | ⭐⭐ |
| Minimal | ⭐ |

This is much easier to justify.

---

# Why is this better?

Because we already know from

- Lighthouse
- OWASP
- Security Headers
- OWASP ZAP

which findings are generally high impact.

We're not inventing percentages.

We're expressing the impact qualitatively.

---

# But suppose you REALLY want percentages.

There is only one defensible way.

Example

Current Performance Score

```
68
```

If user fixes

```
Image Compression
```

Run Lighthouse again.

Suppose

```
82
```

Now

Improvement

```
68 → 82

= +14 points
```

This is **real**.

Not predicted.

Measured.

The same idea can apply to performance because Lighthouse gives a score before and after changes.

However, for security, this is much harder because there is no equivalent "security score" that automatically recalculates after hypothetical fixes.

---

# My recommendation

I would not predict percentages.

I would show something like:

```
Issue

Missing CSP

Priority

Critical

Reason

High exploitability

Expected Security Impact

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐

Performance Impact

—

Implementation Difficulty

Easy

Estimated Time

10–15 min

Reference

OWASP ASVS
```

That is much more defensible.

---

# There is one feature that **would** be novel and feasible

Instead of:

> **"If you fix this, Security +18%"**

show:

> **"If you fix this issue, your Digital Trust Score is expected to improve from 72 → 78."**

Why?

Because **Digital Trust Score is your own scoring framework**.

You define how it is calculated.

Then you can legitimately say:

- Current Trust Score = 72
- After resolving Issue A = 75
- After resolving Issue B = 78

Those improvements are based on **your scoring methodology**, not on invented industry percentages.

That is something you can fully explain and defend in your research paper and viva. I think this is a much stronger and more realistic approach than predicting arbitrary security or performance percentage gains.

# Q1. How will we validate the URL?

There are **3 levels of validation**.

## Level 1 — Syntax Validation (Frontend)

Immediately check if it looks like a URL.

Example

✅ Valid

```

abc

No backend request.

## Level 2 — Reachability (Backend)

```

Looks valid.

But

website doesn't exist.

```
HEAD request
```

or

```

```

```
200

301

# One thing I would change before we proceed to Phase 3

I would **replace "Security Headers" as a separate external tool** with **our own lightweight header analyzer**.

Why?

Because all it really does is inspect the HTTP response headers. We can do that ourselves with a simple Python request:

- Send an HTTPS request.
- Read the response headers.
- Check for the presence of:
  - `Content-Security-Policy`
  - `Strict-Transport-Security`
  - `X-Frame-Options`
  - `X-Content-Type-Options`
  - `Referrer-Policy`
  - `Permissions-Policy`

This has three advantages:

1. **No dependency** on a third-party service.
2. **Faster** execution.
3. You can honestly say in your viva:
   > "We implemented our own HTTP security header assessment module."

So my final Phase 2 stack would actually be:

- ✅ **Lighthouse** (Performance)
- ✅ **OWASP ZAP** (API Security)
- ✅ **Custom Header Analyzer** (Website Security Headers)
- ✅ **SSL/TLS Inspection Module** (Certificate & TLS)

I think this is cleaner, more reliable, and more impressive than relying on too many external services.

# Q1. Which KPIs should we keep?

This is where most students make a mistake.

They keep **100+ metrics** because Lighthouse provides them.

**We should NOT do that.**

We should keep only KPIs that satisfy these three conditions:

- Important for security or performance
- Can influence decision-making
- Easy to explain in a viva

I recommend **22 KPIs**.

---

# A. Performance KPIs (Lighthouse)

| KPI | Why? |
|------|------|
| Performance Score | Overall performance |
| LCP | Core Web Vital |
| CLS | Core Web Vital |
| INP | Core Web Vital |
| FCP | First Paint |
| Speed Index | Loading experience |
| Total Blocking Time | Responsiveness |
| Time To Interactive | User readiness |

Total = **8**

---

# B. Website Security KPIs

| KPI | Why? |
|------|------|
| HTTPS Enabled | Basic security |
| CSP Present | XSS protection |
| HSTS Present | HTTPS enforcement |
| X-Frame-Options | Clickjacking |
| X-Content-Type-Options | MIME sniffing |
| Referrer Policy | Information leakage |
| TLS Version | Encryption strength |
| Certificate Valid | Trust |

Total = **8**

---

# C. API KPIs

| KPI | Why? |
|------|------|
| SQL Injection Found | Critical vulnerability |
| Broken Authentication | OWASP API Top 10 |
| Missing Rate Limiting | API abuse |
| Response Time | Performance |
| HTTPS Used | Secure transport |
| API Error Rate | Reliability |

Total = **6**

---

Total Features

```text
8 + 8 + 6 = 22 KPIs
```

This is perfect.

# Q3. How do we convert KPIs into Feature Vectors?

This is much easier than it sounds.

A feature vector is simply a list of numbers.

Example

Website scan

| KPI | Value |
|------|------|
| Performance Score | 82 |
| LCP | 2.4 |
| CLS | 0.08 |
| HTTPS | Yes |
| CSP | No |
| HSTS | Yes |
| TLS | 1.3 |

Convert

```text
Yes → 1

No → 0
```

Convert TLS

```text
TLS1.0 = 1

TLS1.1 = 2

TLS1.2 = 3

TLS1.3 = 4
```

Final vector

```text
[82,
2.4,
0.08,
1,
0,
1,
4,
...]
```

This is what Random Forest receives.

Notice

The ML model never sees

"CSP"

It sees

0

---

Suppose

API doesn't have

Rate Limiting.

Scanner couldn't determine.

Do

```text
Missing

↓

-1
```

or

```text
NaN
```

Then impute later.

Never leave blank.

# ⚠️ One thing I would change before Phase 4

This is the **only thing I think we should modify**.

Earlier we discussed using **NVD + EPSS + CISA KEV** to train the ML model.

Now look carefully.

Our **Phase 3 feature vector** contains:

- LCP
- CLS
- CSP
- HSTS
- TLS
- SQL Injection
- API Latency

But the **NVD dataset** contains:

- CVSS
- Attack Vector
- Privileges Required
- CWE
- EPSS
- KEV

These are **completely different feature spaces**.

That means **you cannot directly train a model on NVD and expect it to take Lighthouse features as input**.

This is a fundamental mismatch.

## I think we need to resolve this before moving to Phase 4.

We have two realistic options:

### Option A (Recommended)
Train the ML model on **scanner-derived features**. This requires a compatible labeled dataset (we'll need to identify one or derive labels carefully).

### Option B
Use **NVD/EPSS/CISA** not as training data for the scanner output, but as a **knowledge base** that enriches and justifies recommendations.

**I strongly recommend we stop here and resolve this mismatch before designing the AI Decision Support Engine.**

This is probably the most critical architectural decision in the whole project, and fixing it now will save weeks of rework later.

# First, can we directly relate Lighthouse and NVD?

## Short answer:

**No.**

Not in a scientifically valid way.

Because:

Lighthouse says:

```text
LCP = 3.8 s
CLS = 0.12
Performance Score = 74
```

NVD says:

```text
CVE-2025-1234
CVSS = 9.8
Attack Vector = Network
Privileges = None
```

These describe **completely different things**.

There is no dataset in the world that says

```text
LCP = 3.8 sec

↓

CVSS = 8.5
```

So if we artificially join them, **your paper will not be defendable**.

---

# But...

I think there **is** a solution.

And I honestly think this is the architecture we should adopt.

---

# Instead of ONE ML model,

## build TWO intelligence modules.

```text
                 Website/API

                     │

        ┌────────────┴─────────────┐

        ▼                          ▼

 Assessment Engine          Threat Intelligence Engine

(Lighthouse/ZAP)          (NVD+EPSS+CISA)

        │                          │

        └────────────┬─────────────┘

                     ▼

         AI Decision Support Engine

                     ▼

|-----------------|-----|

| Cross-Site Scripting | CWE-79 |
| Missing CSP | CWE-79 (mitigates XSS risk) |
| Weak TLS | CWE-326 |
| Missing HSTS | CWE-319 |
| Broken Authentication | CWE-287 |

This table is **small**, explainable, and entirely feasible for a 3-month project.

3. **Decision Support Layer** → Combines scanner findings, mapped CWE knowledge, ML risk prediction, and LLM explanations.

This keeps:
- your mentor happy because there is a genuine ML component,
- your research paper technically sound,
- and your implementation realistic within three months.

I believe this is the strongest version of the project we've discussed so far.

# Now let's answer the most important question.

## Is this seamless?

**Almost.**

But I still see **one weak point**.

### The weak point

I wrote:

> "Look up CWE-89 in NVD."

That is **not enough**.

Because **NVD is organized around CVEs, not CWEs**.

One CWE (e.g., CWE-89: SQL Injection) can be associated with **thousands of CVEs**, each with different CVSS scores, EPSS scores, and KEV status.

So **instead of mapping to one CVE**, we should build a **small offline Threat Intelligence Index** during development.

Example:

| CWE | Avg CVSS | Avg EPSS | KEV % | Risk Weight |
|------|----------|----------|-------|-------------|
| CWE-89 (SQL Injection) | 9.2 | 0.87 | 41% | Very High |
| CWE-79 (XSS) | 7.8 | 0.43 | 18% | High |
| CWE-287 (Broken Auth) | 9.0 | 0.81 | 36% | Very High |

This table is generated **once** from NVD + EPSS + CISA before deployment.

Then, at runtime:

1. ZAP reports **CWE-89**.
2. Your system instantly looks up **CWE-89** in your Threat Intelligence Index.
3. Those enriched values become additional ML features.
4. The ML model produces the remediation priority.

This solves **all** the previous problems:

- ✅ NVD, EPSS, and CISA are genuinely used.
- ✅ The ML model has meaningful, consistent inputs.
- ✅ ZAP output naturally connects to the datasets.
- ✅ Lighthouse remains relevant as performance evidence.
- ✅ The architecture is one continuous pipeline, not disconnected pieces.

---

## My final opinion

**This is the first architecture in our entire discussion that I would personally be comfortable defending in front of an IEEE reviewer or a project evaluation panel.**

However, before we freeze it, I would do **one verification step**:

> **Confirm that the specific OWASP ZAP alerts we intend to use consistently expose CWE IDs (or another stable mapping field).**

If they do—which they generally do for many vulnerability classes—then I think we have a technically sound, research-worthy, and implementable architecture. If some alerts lack CWE mappings, we can supplement them with a small curated mapping table for those specific cases. That is a manageable engineering task within your timeline.

## Response:
7/17/2026, 9:15:30 AM

Yes. For Review-I, **the PPT should not be a research paper**. It should be **clear, convincing, and concise**. Panel members usually spend only **5–8 minutes** with each group, so every slide should answer one question.

Below is the **final presentation content** that I would personally present if I were in your position.

---

# Slide 1: Title

## AI-Driven Intelligent Security and Performance Assessment System with AI-Based Decision Support

**Team Members**
- Name 1
- Name 2
- Name 3

**Guide**
Dr. __________

---

# Slide 2: Background

## Background

Websites and APIs are the backbone of modern digital services. Organizations rely on various security and performance assessment tools such as Google Lighthouse, OWASP ZAP, and SSL analyzers to evaluate their applications. While these tools effectively detect issues, they often generate lengthy technical reports that require significant manual effort to interpret and prioritize.

As the number of identified issues increases, developers struggle to determine which vulnerabilities should be addressed first and which improvements will have the greatest impact. This results in delayed remediation, inefficient resource allocation, and increased security risks.

---

# Slide 3: Problem Statement

## Problem Statement

Current website and API assessment tools successfully identify security vulnerabilities and performance issues; however, they primarily provide raw technical reports without intelligent decision support.

Developers must manually analyze multiple reports, understand the severity of each issue, and decide the order in which they should be fixed. This process is time-consuming, inconsistent, and often results in delayed mitigation of critical vulnerabilities.

---

# Slide 4: Purpose of the Project

## Purpose

The purpose of this project is to develop an AI-driven decision support system that integrates website security findings, API security findings, and website performance metrics into a unified platform.

Instead of presenting raw reports, the system will intelligently prioritize detected issues, provide explainable recommendations, and generate a structured remediation roadmap to assist developers in making faster and more informed decisions.

---

# Slide 5: Need of the Project

## Why is this project needed?

- Existing tools focus on **issue detection** rather than **decision support**.
- Developers spend considerable time interpreting multiple technical reports.
- Small and medium organizations often lack cybersecurity expertise.
- There is no unified platform that combines website security, API security, and performance assessment with AI-assisted prioritization.
- Explainable recommendations can significantly reduce manual analysis effort.

# Slide 6: Research Gap

## Research Gap

Our literature review identified the following gaps:

- Existing studies primarily focus on **vulnerability detection** rather than remediation prioritization.
- Website security, API security, and performance assessment are generally treated as independent problems.
- Existing tools provide technical reports but lack intelligent decision support.
- Limited research combines security assessment with explainable AI for actionable recommendations.
- Very few frameworks estimate remediation priority based on both technical findings and threat intelligence.

---

# Slide 7: Novelty (USP)

Unlike existing assessment tools, our proposed system:

- Integrates website security, API security, and performance assessment into a single platform.
- Uses an AI-based Decision Support Engine to prioritize remediation instead of simply reporting issues.
- Provides explainable recommendations using Large Language Models (LLMs).
- Generates a structured remediation roadmap to help developers decide what to fix first.
- Enriches security findings using public cybersecurity intelligence (NVD, EPSS, and CISA KEV datasets).

Absolutely. Based on **your project title**, **Review-I stage**, **your PPT**, and the way engineering faculty usually conduct capstone reviews, I would expect the panel to focus on **research justification**, **problem understanding**, **novelty**, and **feasibility**, not implementation details.

Your project is:

> **AI-Driven Intelligent Security and Performance Assessment System**

Below are the **most probable questions** (around 30) that I believe you should prepare.

---

# Section 1: Project Understanding (Most Important)

## 1. Explain your project in 1 minute.

Prepare a simple answer.

> "Our project is an AI-driven platform that analyzes a website or REST API for security and performance issues. Instead of only generating technical reports like existing tools, it intelligently prioritizes the detected issues and provides explainable recommendations to help developers decide what should be fixed first."

---

## 2. What problem are you solving?

Answer should be:

> Existing assessment tools detect issues but do not help developers prioritize remediation. Developers must manually analyze multiple reports, increasing effort and delaying critical fixes.

---

## 3. Why did you choose this problem?

Expect this.

Talk about

- Increasing cyber threats
- Growing dependency on websites/APIs
- Manual analysis
- Research gap

---

## 4. Who faces this problem?

Answer:

- SMEs
- Startup companies
- Freelance developers
- QA Engineers
- DevOps teams

---

## 5. Why is this problem important?

They want justification.

---

# Section 2: Research

## 6. How many papers did you study?

Say honestly.

Example:

> We reviewed six core papers and several supporting papers covering website security, API security, vulnerability prioritization, website performance, and explainable AI.

---

## 7. What research gap did you identify?

Probably the most important question.

Your answer:

> Existing research mainly focuses on detecting vulnerabilities, whereas limited work has been done on intelligently prioritizing remediation by combining website security, API security, and performance findings into a unified decision-support system.

---

## 8. Which paper inspired your work the most?

Mention one.

Explain in two lines.

---

## 9. What novelty are you introducing?

Expect this for sure.

Answer:

- Unified platform
- AI Decision Support
- Prioritized remediation roadmap
- Explainable recommendations

---

## 10. Is your novelty already available?

Very important.

Say

> Existing tools provide reports, but we are focusing on intelligent prioritization and decision support.

---

# Section 3: Existing Systems

Say

- Google Lighthouse
- OWASP ZAP
- Security Headers
- SSL Labs

---

## 12. Then why do we need your project?

Very common.

Answer:

> Existing tools generate independent reports. Our system combines their findings and helps developers decide which issues should be fixed first.

---

## 13. Why can't developers simply use Lighthouse?

Prepare this.

---

## 14. Why can't they simply use ChatGPT?

They may ask.

Good answer:

> ChatGPT depends on the information provided by the user and does not perform actual security or performance assessment. Our system first performs structured assessment using specialized tools and then uses AI for prioritization and explanation.

---

# Section 4: AI

## 15. Why AI?

Answer:

AI helps

- prioritize findings
- reduce manual analysis
- provide explainable recommendations

---

## 16. Why not build a simple dashboard?

Because dashboard

↓

shows information.

AI

↓

helps make decisions.

---

## 17. Is your AI replacing developers?

No.

It assists decision-making.

---

## 18. Why is Explainable AI important?

Security professionals must understand

WHY

AI suggested something.

- NVD
- EPSS
- CISA KEV

vulnerability database

exploit probability

known exploited vulnerabilities

Reliable.

Standard.

Research accepted.

we are using

existing tools

instead of building scanners.

## Response:
7/23/2026, 9:31:01 PM

Perfect. These are exactly the questions I think the panel is most likely to ask. I'll give you **natural, human-like answers** that sound like a student who understands the project—not memorized textbook definitions.

---

# 1. Explain your project in 1 minute.

**Answer:**

"Our project, **AI-Driven Security & Performance Assessment Platform**, aims to provide a unified platform for assessing both website and REST API security along with website performance. Existing tools such as Google Lighthouse and OWASP ZAP can detect issues, but they generate separate technical reports that developers have to analyze manually. Our platform brings these assessments together and uses AI to prioritize the detected issues, provide explainable recommendations, and generate a remediation roadmap so that developers know what to fix first."

---

# 2. Why AI?

**Answer:**

"We are not using AI just to detect vulnerabilities because existing tools already do that well. We are using AI to help developers make better decisions. The AI analyzes the assessment findings, prioritizes the issues based on their importance, and provides explainable recommendations. This reduces manual effort and helps developers focus on the most critical issues first."

---

# 3. What research gap did you identify?

**Answer:**

"From our literature review, we found that most existing research focuses on detecting vulnerabilities or measuring website performance separately. Very few studies combine website security, API security, and performance assessment into one platform. We also found that existing tools generate technical reports but provide limited decision support for prioritizing remediation. This is the main research gap our project aims to address."

---

# 4. Why Website + API together?

**Answer:**

"Modern web applications consist of both a frontend website and backend APIs. Even if a website performs well, insecure APIs can still expose sensitive data. Similarly, a secure API alone cannot ensure a good user experience if the website performs poorly. Assessing both together provides a more complete view of an application's overall quality."

---

# 5. What is your novelty?

**Answer:**

"Our novelty is not in building another security scanner. Instead, we integrate website security, API security, and performance assessment into a single platform. We use AI to prioritize the detected issues and provide explainable recommendations instead of just displaying raw technical reports."

---

# 6. Who are your target users?

**Answer:**

"Our primary target users are startups, SMEs, freelance developers, QA engineers, and DevOps teams. These organizations often have limited cybersecurity expertise and resources, so a unified and intelligent assessment platform can help them identify and prioritize issues more efficiently."

---

# 7. Why will people use your project instead of Lighthouse?

**Answer:**

"Google Lighthouse is an excellent tool for website performance, but it mainly focuses on performance, accessibility, SEO, and best practices. It does not perform comprehensive API security assessment or intelligently prioritize remediation. Our platform combines multiple assessment results into one dashboard and helps developers decide what should be fixed first through AI-assisted recommendations."

---

# 8. Can you complete this project in 3 months?

**Answer:**

"Yes. The project is feasible because we are using existing open-source assessment tools rather than building scanners from scratch. Our work is mainly focused on integrating these tools, developing the AI-assisted decision support module, building the dashboard, and validating the system. We have also divided the work among three team members."

---

# 9. Why are you using NVD, EPSS, and CISA KEV datasets?

**Answer:**

"We selected these datasets because they are publicly available, reliable, and widely used in cybersecurity research. NVD provides detailed vulnerability information, EPSS estimates the likelihood of exploitation, and CISA KEV lists vulnerabilities that are actively exploited in real-world attacks. Together, they provide valuable threat intelligence to support our AI-assisted decision-making."

---

# 10. ChatGPT can also suggest fixes. Why do we need your project?

**Answer:**

"ChatGPT depends entirely on the information provided by the user. It does not perform actual website or API assessment. Our platform first collects structured security and performance findings using specialized assessment tools. AI then analyzes these verified findings and provides recommendations based on actual assessment results, making the output more reliable and actionable."

---

# 11. Why is your project needed?

**Answer:**

"Although many assessment tools exist, developers still spend significant time switching between different tools and manually analyzing reports. Our project reduces this effort by integrating multiple assessments into one platform and providing intelligent prioritization."

---

# 12. Why is this project important?

**Answer:**

"As organizations increasingly rely on websites and APIs, maintaining both security and performance has become essential. Delayed remediation of vulnerabilities can lead to security breaches, while poor performance affects user experience and business reputation. Our project aims to address both challenges together."

---

# 13. Why did you choose this topic?

**Answer:**

"We chose this topic because it addresses a real industry problem. During our literature review, we observed that most existing solutions focus only on issue detection. We wanted to build a system that goes one step further by helping developers make better remediation decisions."

---

# 14. Which paper influenced your project the most?

**Answer:**

"The paper *'A Survey on Vulnerability Prioritization: Taxonomy, Metrics, and Research Challenges'* had the greatest influence on our project.
