## Prompt:
7/23/2026, 9:55:26 PM

what is black box scores

## Response:
7/23/2026, 9:55:27 PM

This is actually an important term because you have used it in your **Novelty** slide:

> **"Explainable AI (LLM-based) recommendations, not black-box scores."** `Review-1_On_Provided_Template (1).pptx`

You should know exactly what it means in case the panel asks.

---

## What is a Black-Box Score?

A **black-box score** is an output where the system gives you a result **without explaining how or why it reached that result**.

### Example

Suppose a tool shows:

```
Security Score = 42/100
```

That's all.

You don't know:

- Why is it 42?
- Which issue reduced the score?
- Which issue is most critical?
- What should you fix first?

The system gives a score, but the reasoning is hidden.

This is called a **black-box system**.

---

## What is Explainable AI?

Now imagine your system gives:

```
Security Score: High Risk

Reason:
• SQL Injection detected (Critical)
• Missing Content Security Policy
• Weak TLS configuration

Priority:
1. Fix SQL Injection
2. Enable CSP
3. Upgrade TLS

Expected Impact:
Security Improvement: High
Performance Impact: Minimal
```

Here the user knows:

- Why the score is high.
- Which issue caused it.
- What to fix first.
- Why that recommendation was made.

This is **Explainable AI (XAI).**

---

## Very simple analogy

### Black Box

Teacher says:

> "You got 45 marks."

Nothing else.

---

### Explainable AI

Teacher says:

> "You got 45 because:
> - 10 marks lost in Question 2
> - 15 marks lost in Question 4
> - Your concepts were correct but calculations were wrong."

Now you understand **why**.

That's Explainable AI.

---

## In your project

Instead of saying

```
Risk Score = 85
```

your platform will say

```
High Risk

Reason:
• SQL Injection is a critical vulnerability.
• It has a high exploitation probability.
• It can expose sensitive database information.

Recommendation:
Use parameterized queries and input validation.

Priority:
Fix immediately.
```

Now the developer trusts the recommendation.

---

## If the panel asks

### What do you mean by "not black-box scores"?

You can answer:

> "A black-box score simply provides a final result without explaining how it was calculated. Our project focuses on Explainable AI, where the system not only prioritizes issues but also explains the reasoning behind each recommendation. This helps developers understand why a particular issue is important and what action should be taken."

---

### One small suggestion

I would slightly change the wording on your **Novelty** slide.

Instead of:

> **Explainable AI (LLM-based) recommendations, not black-box scores**

write:

> **Explainable AI (LLM-based) recommendations with transparent reasoning**

This is more professional, easier for a Review-I audience to understand, and avoids introducing the term "black-box" unless you explain it during the presentation.

## Prompt:
7/23/2026, 9:58:02 PM

why we are using Gemini for explainable AI

## Response:
7/23/2026, 9:58:03 PM

This is **one of the most expected viva questions**, and your answer should be very clear.

---

## Question:
### **Why are you using Gemini in your project?**

### Answer (30–40 seconds)

> "We are using Gemini to provide **Explainable AI** capabilities. Existing assessment tools can identify security and performance issues, but they present technical findings that are often difficult for developers, especially beginners, to understand. Gemini helps convert these structured findings into clear, human-readable explanations, explains why an issue is important, suggests possible remediation steps, and justifies the priority assigned to each issue."

---

# What exactly does Gemini do in our project?

It **does not scan websites**.

It **does not detect vulnerabilities**.

It **does not train the ML model**.

Instead, it acts as an **AI assistant** after the analysis is complete.

### Pipeline

```text
Website / API
       │
       ▼
Assessment Tools
(Lighthouse, ZAP, etc.)
       │
       ▼
Security & Performance Findings
       │
       ▼
AI Decision Engine
(Prioritizes Issues)
       │
       ▼
⭐ Gemini
(Explains the Results)
       │
       ▼
Dashboard & PDF Report
```

---

# Example

Suppose ZAP detects

```text
SQL Injection
Risk: High
```

Without Gemini

The dashboard shows

```text
Priority: High
```

That's all.

---

With Gemini

The dashboard shows

```text
Priority: High

Reason:
SQL Injection allows attackers to manipulate database queries and may expose sensitive information.

Recommendation:
Use parameterized queries and validate user inputs.

Why fix first?
This vulnerability can directly compromise confidential data and is commonly exploited.
```

Now the developer understands **why**.

---

# Why not simply show the scanner report?

Because scanner reports are

- technical
- lengthy
- difficult for beginners
- spread across multiple tools

Gemini converts them into

- simple language
- actionable recommendations
- prioritized explanations

---

# Does Gemini make the decisions?

**No.**

This is very important.

Say this if asked:

> "Gemini does not independently decide the priority of vulnerabilities. The prioritization is performed by our AI Decision Support Engine. Gemini's role is to explain the findings and recommendations in a human-readable manner."

This makes your system more trustworthy.

---

# Why Gemini specifically?

You can say:

> "Gemini has strong natural language understanding and generation capabilities. It helps transform structured technical findings into concise, contextual, and developer-friendly explanations, improving the usability of our platform."

You don't need to claim Gemini is better than every other LLM.

---

# If the panel asks:

### "Why not ChatGPT?"

Answer:

> "Any capable LLM could provide explanations. We selected Gemini because it offers strong language generation capabilities, is easily accessible through APIs, and integrates well into our planned technology stack. Our research contribution is not the choice of LLM but how we combine assessment findings, AI-based prioritization, and explainable recommendations into one workflow."

This is a balanced answer that focuses attention back on **your novelty**, not the LLM itself.

---

## One correction I would make to your project description

From everything we've discussed over the past few weeks, I would avoid saying:

> **"Gemini is our AI."**

Instead, consistently say:

> **"Our AI Decision Support Engine prioritizes issues, while Gemini provides explainable recommendations and natural-language justification."**

This separation is technically correct, easier to defend in a viva, and clearly communicates that the innovation lies in your decision-support workflow—not simply in calling an LLM API.

This slide is good because it shows the complete tech stack. The panel will definitely ask:

> **"Why did you choose this technology and not some other?"**

Don't answer like "because it's popular." Give a practical reason related to your project.

---

# 1. Frontend – React.js

### Why React.js?

> "We chose React.js because our platform requires an interactive dashboard with dynamic updates, charts, filters, and reports. React's component-based architecture makes the UI modular, reusable, and easier to maintain."

### Why not Angular?

- Angular is more complex and has a steeper learning curve.
- React is lightweight and sufficient for our project timeline.

### Why not HTML/CSS/JavaScript only?

- Difficult to manage large applications.
- React provides reusable components and better state management.

---

# 2. Tailwind CSS

### Why Tailwind CSS?

> "Tailwind CSS helps us build a responsive and modern user interface quickly using utility classes. It speeds up development and reduces the need for writing custom CSS."

### Why not Bootstrap?

- Bootstrap often produces similar-looking websites.
- Tailwind offers greater flexibility in customization while remaining lightweight.

---

# 3. Backend – Python

### Why Python?

> "Python has excellent support for AI, machine learning, cybersecurity tools, and data processing. Since our project combines assessment tools with AI, Python provides the best ecosystem."

### Why not Java?

- Java is excellent for enterprise applications.
- However, Python has stronger libraries for AI and integrates easily with cybersecurity tools like OWASP ZAP.

---

# 4. FastAPI

### Why FastAPI?

> "FastAPI is a modern Python framework that allows us to build REST APIs quickly with high performance. It also integrates well with AI models and provides automatic API documentation."

### Why not Flask?

- Flask is simple but requires additional setup for many features.
- FastAPI is faster and better suited for API-based applications.

### Why not Django?

- Django is a full-stack framework with many built-in features that we don't need.
- FastAPI is more lightweight for our backend services.

---

# 5. Database – PostgreSQL

### Why PostgreSQL?

> "PostgreSQL is an open-source relational database known for reliability, scalability, and strong support for structured data. It is well suited for storing assessment reports, user information, and analysis history."

### Why not MySQL?

- Both are good.
- PostgreSQL provides better support for advanced queries and future scalability.

### Why not MongoDB?

- Our data (users, reports, scans) is relational and structured.
- A relational database is a better fit.

---

# 6. Scikit-learn

### Why Scikit-learn?

> "Scikit-learn provides reliable implementations of machine learning algorithms and is easy to use for research and prototyping. It is sufficient for our AI decision-support module."

### Why not TensorFlow or PyTorch?

- Those frameworks are mainly used for deep learning.
- Our project focuses on traditional machine learning, where Scikit-learn is simpler and more appropriate.

---

# 7. Google Gemini API

### Why Gemini?

> "Gemini is used only for Explainable AI. It converts technical findings into simple, human-readable explanations and remediation recommendations. This improves usability without replacing our decision-support logic."

### Why not ChatGPT?

> "Any capable LLM could perform this role. We selected Gemini because it provides accessible APIs, strong natural language capabilities, and integrates well with our planned technology stack."

---

# 8. Google Lighthouse

### Why Lighthouse?

> "Lighthouse is Google's official open-source tool for measuring website performance, accessibility, SEO, and best practices. It is widely accepted, reliable, and provides standardized performance metrics."

### Why not GTmetrix?

- GTmetrix offers useful analysis but many advanced features require a paid subscription.
- Lighthouse is free, open source, and directly supported by Google.

---

# 9. OWASP ZAP

### Why OWASP ZAP?

> "OWASP ZAP is one of the most widely used open-source web and API security testing tools. It detects common vulnerabilities such as SQL Injection, XSS, and security misconfigurations."

### Why not Burp Suite?

- Burp Suite Professional is commercial software.
- OWASP ZAP is free, open source, and sufficient for academic research.

---

# 10. SSL/TLS Inspection

### Why?

> "SSL/TLS inspection verifies whether secure communication is properly implemented by checking certificate validity, encryption protocols, and TLS configuration."

### Why is it important?

- Ensures secure communication.
- Detects weak or outdated encryption.

---

# 11. HTTP Header Analysis

### Why?

> "HTTP security headers such as Content Security Policy (CSP), HSTS, and X-Frame-Options help protect websites against common attacks like XSS and clickjacking. Checking these headers provides a quick assessment of basic web security."

---

# 12. NVD Dataset

### Why NVD?

> "NVD is the official U.S. National Vulnerability Database containing standardized information about publicly disclosed vulnerabilities, including CVE and CVSS details. It serves as our primary vulnerability knowledge source."

### Why not create our own dataset?

- Time constraints.
- NVD is trusted and continuously updated.

---

# 13. EPSS Dataset

### Why EPSS?

> "EPSS estimates the probability that a vulnerability will be exploited in the real world. It helps distinguish vulnerabilities that are theoretically severe from those that are actually likely to be attacked."

### Why is this useful?

Because not every high-CVSS vulnerability is actively exploited.

---

# 14. CISA KEV

### Why CISA KEV?

> "The CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog contains vulnerabilities that are confirmed to be actively exploited. It provides real-world threat intelligence to strengthen prioritization."

### Why use KEV if we already have NVD?

- NVD tells us what the vulnerability is.
- EPSS estimates exploitation likelihood.
- CISA KEV confirms active exploitation.

Together they provide complementary information.

---

# ⭐ The panel's favorite question

## "Why are you using existing assessment tools instead of building your own?"

### Answer

> "Developing a reliable security scanner from scratch would require significant time and expertise. Existing tools like Lighthouse and OWASP ZAP are already well validated and widely used. Our research contribution is not in recreating scanners but in integrating their outputs and providing AI-assisted decision support for remediation prioritization."

This answer clearly positions your work as an **integration and intelligence problem**, which matches your research gap and keeps the focus on your project's actual contribution.

### **How are you training the AI module?** *(Review-I Appropriate Answer)*

We will train our AI module using publicly available cybersecurity datasets such as **NVD (National Vulnerability Database)**, **EPSS (Exploit Prediction Scoring System)**, and **CISA KEV (Known Exploited Vulnerabilities)**. These datasets will first be cleaned, preprocessed, and merged using common vulnerability identifiers (CVE IDs) to create a unified dataset containing features such as CVSS severity, exploit probability, vulnerability category, attack vector, attack complexity, and exploitation status.

To identify the most suitable machine learning model, we will train and compare multiple algorithms, primarily **Random Forest** and **XGBoost**. Their performance will be evaluated using standard metrics such as **Accuracy, Precision, Recall, F1-Score, and ROC-AUC**. Based on the experimental results, we will either select the best-performing model or, if combining both models improves performance, develop a **hybrid ensemble model** for our AI Decision Support Engine.

The trained model will then prioritize detected vulnerabilities based on their severity and exploitation likelihood. Finally, **Google Gemini** will be used to generate human-readable, explainable recommendations so that developers can easily understand **why** an issue is important and **what** corrective action should be taken.

## Response:
7/23/2026, 11:31:06 PM

Absolutely. Since this is a **Review-I presentation**, the script should sound **confident, conversational, and natural**—not like you're reading from the slides.

I've written it as if **you are the first presenter**, covering **Slides 1, 2, 3, 8, and 9**.

---

# 🎤 Slide 1 – Title

> **Good morning respected panel members and our mentor.**
>
> My name is **Pal Rathore**, and along with my teammates **Priyal Rathore** and **Vaishnavi Gandhi**, we are here to present our capstone project proposal titled **"AI-Driven Security and Performance Assessment Platform."**
>
> The main aim of our project is to develop an intelligent platform that helps developers assess the security and performance of websites and REST APIs in a single place. Rather than just generating technical reports, our platform will assist developers by intelligently prioritizing issues and providing explainable recommendations to support better decision-making.
>
> Now, I'd like to begin with the introduction of our project.

---

# 🎤 Slide 2 – Introduction

> Today, almost every organization depends on websites and APIs to deliver its digital services. Whether it's an e-commerce website, a banking application, or a food delivery app, both security and performance play a critical role in ensuring reliability and a good user experience.
>
> Existing assessment tools are capable of identifying security vulnerabilities and performance issues. However, these tools generate separate technical reports, and developers still have to manually analyze them before deciding what needs immediate attention.
>
> This manual process is time-consuming and often leads to delayed remediation, especially for small teams that lack dedicated cybersecurity experts.
>
> Therefore, we identified the need for an intelligent unified platform that not only performs assessment but also helps developers make faster and more informed remediation decisions.

---

# 🎤 Slide 3 – Problem Statement

> Based on our literature review, we identified that the major challenge is **not detecting vulnerabilities**, because many existing tools already perform that effectively.
>
> The real challenge begins **after detection**.
>
> Developers often receive multiple security and performance reports from different tools, and they have to manually analyze them, determine which issues are critical, and decide the order in which they should be fixed.
>
> This process increases developer workload, delays risk mitigation, and often requires experienced cybersecurity professionals.
>
> Therefore, our research problem is:
>
> **"Existing website and API assessment tools lack an affordable, integrated, and intelligent decision support system, leading to fragmented analysis, inefficient prioritization, increased developer workload, delayed risk mitigation, and higher operational costs."**
>
> To address this gap, we propose an AI-driven assessment platform that combines multiple assessments into one intelligent workflow.

---

# 🎤 Slide 8 – Proposed Solution

> To solve this problem, we propose a unified assessment platform with a simple and structured workflow.
>
> First, the user provides either a **website URL** or an **API endpoint** for analysis.
>
> The platform then performs security and performance assessment using integrated tools.
>
> The collected findings are passed to our **AI Decision Support Engine**, which analyzes the results, prioritizes the identified issues, and generates explainable recommendations.
>
> Finally, the user receives an interactive dashboard along with a downloadable PDF report containing prioritized issues and suggested remediation steps.
>
> Our objective is not to replace existing assessment tools, but to enhance their usefulness by providing intelligent decision support.

---

# 🎤 Slide 9 – Scope of the Project

> Coming to the scope of our project, our system will primarily focus on **website performance assessment, website security assessment, REST API security assessment, AI-assisted prioritization, dashboard visualization, and PDF report generation.**
>
> At the same time, we have clearly defined the limitations of our project.
>
> Our current scope does not include source code analysis, active penetration testing, malware detection, cloud infrastructure assessment, or mobile application security.
>
> The primary target users for our platform are **startups, small and medium enterprises, freelance developers, QA engineers, DevOps engineers, and students**, as these users can benefit the most from an affordable and easy-to-use intelligent assessment platform.

---

# 🎤 Smooth Transition

After Slide 9, end naturally:

> **"This was the overview of our proposed solution and its scope. Now, I'll hand over the presentation to my teammate, who will explain the technologies used, feasibility analysis, and project planning."**

---

# ⭐ Presentation Tips

- **Smile when introducing yourselves.**
- **Don't read directly from the slide**—the slide is for the audience, not for you.
- Maintain eye contact with the panel.
- Pause briefly after important points like **"The real challenge begins after detection."** This adds emphasis.
- Aim for **45–60 seconds per slide**, except the title slide (20–30 seconds).

This script will take about **4–5 minutes** for your portion and sounds natural, confident, and appropriate for a capstone Review-I presentation. Good luck!

## Response:
7/28/2026, 9:48:37 AM

Yes. Now we're at the stage where we should stop talking in abstract terms and **design the actual system**. I'm also going to be honest with you:

> **Phase 3 will decide whether your AI module makes sense or looks like an afterthought.**

Earlier we discussed many possibilities, but after everything we've learned, I think we should **slightly simplify the architecture** so it's implementable in **3 months**.

---

# PHASE 3 – Data Processing & Feature Engineering

## Goal of Phase 3

The goal of this phase is to **convert the raw outputs generated by Lighthouse, OWASP ZAP, SSL inspection, and HTTP Header Analysis into a single structured dataset that can be given to the AI model.**

The AI **cannot understand PDF reports or JSON reports directly**. It needs structured numerical features.

So Phase 3 acts as the **bridge** between assessment tools and the AI.

---

# Complete Flow

```
Lighthouse
        \
OWASP ZAP \
SSL Check   \
Header Check \
             ↓
      Raw Reports (JSON/XML)
             ↓
     Data Extraction Script
             ↓
      KPI Selection
             ↓
   Data Cleaning & Standardization
             ↓
 Dataset Mapping (NVD + EPSS + KEV)
             ↓
 Feature Vector
             ↓
 AI Model
```

---

# Step 1 – Collect Tool Outputs

Phase 2 already generates reports.

For example,

### Lighthouse

Returns

- Performance Score
- Accessibility Score
- Best Practices
- SEO
- Core Web Vitals

---

### OWASP ZAP

Returns

- SQL Injection
- Cross Site Scripting
- Missing Authentication
- Sensitive Data Exposure
- Security Misconfiguration

---

### SSL Check

Returns

- SSL Validity
- TLS Version
- Cipher Strength

---

### HTTP Header Analysis

Returns

- CSP
- HSTS
- X-Frame-Options
- X-Content-Type-Options

These are still raw reports.

---

# Step 2 – KPI Selection

We do **not** use every field.

Only meaningful KPIs.

Example

| Tool | KPIs Kept |
|---------|----------------|
| Lighthouse | Performance, Accessibility, Best Practices, SEO |
| ZAP | Number of High, Medium, Low Alerts |
| SSL | TLS Version, Certificate Status |
| Headers | Missing Security Headers |

Everything else is ignored.

Why?

Because irrelevant features reduce ML performance.

---

# Step 3 – Extract KPIs

Now we write Python scripts.

Example

```
Lighthouse JSON

↓

performanceScore

↓

Extract only

Performance = 82
```

Similarly

```
ZAP JSON

↓

Alerts

↓

High Risk = 3

Medium Risk = 5

Low Risk = 9
```

Python simply reads JSON fields.

No AI here.

Only parsing.

---

# Step 4 – Standardization

Now we have

```
Performance = 82

High Alerts = 3

TLS = TLS1.2

Missing CSP = Yes
```

Different tools use different formats.

We convert everything into one common format.

Example

```
TLS1.2

↓

1
```

```
Missing CSP

↓

1

Present

↓

0
```

```
Certificate Valid

↓

1

Invalid

↓

0
```

Now every feature is machine-readable.

---

# Step 5 – Dataset Mapping

This is where your **public datasets** enter the project.

Suppose ZAP detects

```
SQL Injection
```

Now we search the datasets using the vulnerability type or associated **CVE** (when available).

From:

### NVD

We obtain

- CVSS Score
- Attack Vector
- Attack Complexity
- Severity

---

From:

### EPSS

We obtain

- Exploit Probability

Example

```
0.83
```

Meaning

83% chance of exploitation.

---

From:

### CISA KEV

We check

```
Is this vulnerability actively exploited?

Yes

↓

1

No

↓

0
```

Now our feature set becomes richer.

Instead of only saying

```
SQL Injection
```

we know

```
CVSS = 9.8

EPSS = 0.81

KEV = Yes
```

This is valuable information for prioritization.

---

# Step 6 – Create Feature Vector

Now we combine everything into one structured record.

Example

| Feature | Value |
|---------|-------|
| Performance Score | 82 |
| Accessibility | 90 |
| SEO | 88 |
| High Alerts | 3 |
| Medium Alerts | 5 |
| Missing CSP | 1 |
| TLS Valid | 1 |
| CVSS | 9.8 |
| EPSS | 0.81 |
| KEV | 1 |

Now everything is numeric.

The ML model can finally process it.

---

# Step 7 – Store Feature Vector

Instead of giving it directly to AI,

store it first.

Example

```
Website_ID

↓

Extracted Features

↓

PostgreSQL
```

Benefits

- Easy retraining
- Easy debugging
- Historical analysis

---

# Output of Phase 3

The final output is:

```
{
Performance : 82

Accessibility : 90

High Alerts : 3

Missing CSP : 1

CVSS : 9.8

EPSS : 0.81

KEV : 1

...
}
```

This becomes the **input to the AI Decision Support Engine**.

---

# Why Phase 3 is Important

Without Phase 3,

```
Raw Reports

↓

AI
```

❌ Impossible.

With Phase 3,

```
Raw Reports

↓

Feature Extraction

↓

Feature Engineering

↓

AI
```

✅ Correct ML pipeline.

---

# Is this feasible in 3 months?

Yes.

Because you are **not creating data from scratch**.

You are only:

- Reading JSON reports.
- Extracting selected KPIs.
- Looking up additional information from public datasets.
- Creating a structured feature table.

All of these are straightforward engineering tasks and realistic for a three-member team.

## One thing I would change before moving to Phase 4

# Doubt 1

**No. We should NOT physically merge them into one huge dataset.**

I changed my recommendation after thinking about implementation.

Because:

- CISA KEV is much smaller.
- Keeping them permanently merged is difficult to maintain and unnecessary.

Instead, think of them as **three lookup databases**.

## Better Architecture

```text

        ┌────────────┼────────────┐
        ▼            ▼            ▼
      NVD         EPSS        CISA KEV
        │            │            │
        └────────────┼────────────┘
                     ▼
        Threat Intelligence Record
                     ▼
          Feature Vector for AI
```

So we are **not creating one massive merged dataset**.

We are querying each dataset independently.

↓

EPSS = 0.83

Found

↓

Actively Exploited = Yes

{

This gets appended to your extracted features.

# Let's ask the real question

## What is our AI actually predicting?

Before choosing a dataset, we need to know the prediction target.

Possible outputs are:

- Risk Score?
- Trust Score?
- Priority?

- Critical / High / Medium / Low?

When a user scans a website, we actually have these features:

| Feature | Source |
|----------|--------|
| Performance Score | Lighthouse |
| Accessibility | Lighthouse |
| SEO | Lighthouse |
| Best Practices | Lighthouse |
| High Alerts | ZAP |
| Medium Alerts | ZAP |
| Low Alerts | ZAP |
| Missing CSP | Header Check |
| Missing HSTS | Header Check |
| TLS Version | SSL Check |
| Certificate Valid | SSL Check |

These are the **real runtime inputs**.

So if we want ML, **our training dataset must also contain these kinds of features.**

---

# Here's the problem

Do NVD, EPSS, or CISA KEV contain:

- Performance Score ❌
- SEO ❌
- Accessibility ❌
- Missing CSP ❌
- High Alerts ❌

No.

This is the mismatch you noticed.

# So we have two possible architectures.

## Option A (our current idea)

Train on NVD/EPSS/KEV.

Predict using Lighthouse/ZAP.

❌ Impossible.

Feature mismatch.

## Option B

Train on **the same features produced by our tools.**

This is correct.

**Where do we get such a dataset?**

# This is where the novelty can actually come in.

Instead of finding a ready-made dataset, we build one.

Not manually.

Automatically.

---

Imagine we collect 500 websites.

For each website:

Run Lighthouse.

Run ZAP.

Run SSL check.

Run Header check.

Store all outputs.

Example:

| Website | Perf | SEO | High Alerts | Missing CSP | TLS | ... |
|----------|------|-----|-------------|-------------|-----|-----|
| A | 91 | 88 | 1 | 0 | 1 | ... |
| B | 54 | 72 | 7 | 1 | 0 | ... |
| C | 83 | 95 | 0 | 0 | 1 | ... |

Now we have **exactly the same features** that will appear during prediction.

---

# But then another question:

Who gives the labels?

Exactly.

This is the next challenge.

For supervised learning we need labels.

For example:

```
Priority = High
```

or

```
Trust Score = 82
```

Who decides that?

Not us.

Otherwise it's subjective.

---

# So I think we need to rethink the AI objective.

There are three realistic directions.

### Option 1 - Supervised Learning

Train with labeled examples.

Requires labels.

Hardest.

---

### Option 2 - Unsupervised Learning

Train without labels.

Cluster websites into:

- Low Risk
- Medium Risk
- High Risk

No labels needed.

Novel but different.

---

### Option 3 - Hybrid Scoring + ML

This is the one I think is strongest.

Here's the idea:

1. Collect tool outputs for many websites.
2. Create a **risk score using a transparent formula** (based on OWASP severity, CVSS where available, performance penalties, etc.).
3. Use that calculated score as the target label.
4. Train the ML model to predict that score from the tool features.

Now:

Training input = Runtime input ✅

Training output = Risk Score ✅

Prediction output = Risk Score ✅

Everything matches.

---

# But here's something even more important.

I'm going to challenge one assumption we've carried since the beginning.

**Do we actually need an ML model?**

Not because ML is bad.

Because your mentor specifically wants **AI/ML**, but the best AI is one that solves the problem naturally.

If we force ML where it doesn't fit, the project becomes weak.

If we use ML where it genuinely learns from historical assessment data and predicts an overall risk or remediation priority, then it becomes meaningful.

---

# I think we should stop here and answer one foundational question before designing Phase 4:

> **What exactly should our AI predict?**

Once we lock that answer, everything else becomes straightforward:

- What dataset we need.
- Whether we need labels.
- Whether supervised or unsupervised learning is appropriate.
- How Phase 3 should generate features.
- How Phase 4 should train the model.

## My suggestion

Let's spend time designing the **ML problem itself** before writing any architecture.

We'll define:

1. **Input features** (what the model receives).
2. **Target/output** (what the model predicts).
3. **Training dataset** (where it comes from).
4. **Inference flow** (how a scanned website is processed).

If these four pieces are correct, the rest of the project will be technically sound and your **AI novelty will be defensible** in both the project and the research paper. I would not move forward until we've nailed this.

Good. Now I'm going to give you **the final architecture** that I would personally approve if I were your project guide.

I will also point out one important modification because there is **one place where your current idea can fail**.

---

# First, the Goal of the AI

Our AI **does not analyze websites.**

Existing tools already do that.

Our AI only answers:

> **"Given this detected vulnerability, how critical is it and how should it be prioritized?"**

Once we fix this objective, everything becomes consistent.

---

# STEP 1 - Train the AI Model (Offline)

This happens **only once** before deployment.

### We download three public datasets:

- NVD
- EPSS
- CISA KEV

---

### Step 1.1 - Merge them

All three contain **CVE IDs**, so we merge them using:

```text
CVE ID
```

Example:

| CVE | CVSS | Attack Vector | EPSS | KEV |
|------|-------|---------------|------|-----|
| CVE-2023-1111 | 9.8 | Network | 0.91 | Yes |

This is easy using pandas.

---

### Step 1.2 - Select Features

Keep only useful columns.

Example:

- CVSS Score
- Attack Vector
- Attack Complexity
- Privileges Required
- User Interaction
- Confidentiality Impact
- Integrity Impact
- Availability Impact
- EPSS Score
- KEV Status

These become the **input features** of our ML model.

---

### Step 1.3 - Create Labels

This is the only missing part.

Since these datasets do not contain a "Priority" column, we derive labels using an accepted rule.

Example:

| Condition | Label |
|-----------|-------|
| CVSS ≥ 9 OR KEV = Yes | Critical |
| CVSS 7-8.9 | High |
| CVSS 4-6.9 | Medium |
| Else | Low |

These become our training labels.

## Response:
8/3/2026, 8:34:05 PM

Excellent. I think we've finally reached a point where we should **freeze the architecture**. From now on, every implementation decision should follow this design.

One thing I have changed from our earlier discussions is this:

> **We will NOT train the model on Lighthouse or ZAP outputs.**
>
> We will train it only on **public cybersecurity datasets (NVD + EPSS + CISA KEV)** because that satisfies your constraints and keeps the ML pipeline technically correct.

---

# PHASE 3 - Mapping & Pre-Feature Engineering

## Objective

The objective of this phase is to convert the vulnerabilities detected by the assessment tools into the **same feature format** that was used to train the machine learning model.

The AI model **does not receive the entire website report**. It only receives structured vulnerability features.

---

# Step 3.1 - Receive Security Assessment Results

The assessment phase (already completed) provides security findings from tools such as:

- OWASP ZAP
- SSL/TLS Inspection
- HTTP Security Header Analysis

Example findings:

```
• Apache HTTP Server 2.4.49 detected
• Remote Code Execution vulnerability
• SQL Injection
• Missing CSP
• Weak TLS Configuration
```

At this stage, these are only raw findings.

---

# Step 3.2 - Categorize Findings

Now we divide all findings into two categories.

## Category A - CVE-Based Findings

These findings are associated with a specific software product and version.

Example:

```
Apache HTTP Server 2.4.49
Log4j 2.14
Spring Framework
```

These can be mapped to public vulnerability datasets.

---

## Category B - Configuration Findings

These findings do not have a CVE.

Example:

```
Missing CSP
Missing HSTS
Weak Cookie Flags
Directory Listing
```

These will later be handled using rule-based recommendations and **will not be sent to the ML model**.

This avoids forcing machine learning where no training data exists.

---

# Step 3.3 - Identify Product & Version

For every CVE-based finding, extract:

- Product Name
- Software Version
- Technology Stack

Example

```
Product : Apache HTTP Server
Version : 2.4.49
```

This information becomes the search key.

---

# Step 3.4 - Map to Public Datasets

Now search the three public datasets.

---

## Dataset 1 - NVD

Search using:

```
Product Name
+
Version
```

Extract only the required features:

- CVE ID
- CVSS Score
- Attack Vector
- Attack Complexity
- Privileges Required
- User Interaction
- Confidentiality Impact
- Integrity Impact
- Availability Impact

---

## Dataset 2 - EPSS

Using the same CVE ID,

extract:

- EPSS Score (Probability of Exploitation)

---

## Dataset 3 - CISA KEV

Using the same CVE ID,

check:

- Is the vulnerability actively exploited?

Output:

```
Yes = 1

No = 0
```

---

# Step 3.5 - Create Unified Feature Record

The extracted information is combined into one structured feature record.

Example:

| Feature | Value |
|----------|-------|
| CVSS Score | 9.8 |
| Attack Vector | Network |
| Attack Complexity | Low |
| Privileges Required | None |
| User Interaction | None |
| Confidentiality Impact | High |
| Integrity Impact | High |
| Availability Impact | High |
| EPSS Score | 0.93 |
| KEV Status | 1 |

This is the **exact input format** expected by the machine learning model.

---

# Step 3.6 - Store Processed Features

The generated feature records are stored in PostgreSQL for:

- Prediction
- Future analysis
- Report generation
- Model retraining (if required)

---

# Output of Phase 3

The output is a clean and standardized feature vector.

Example

```
CVSS = 9.8
Attack Vector = Network
Attack Complexity = Low
Privileges Required = None
EPSS = 0.93
KEV = 1
...
```

This is now ready for the AI model.

---

# PHASE 4 - Model Training

## Objective

Train a machine learning model using publicly available cybersecurity datasets so that it can intelligently prioritize vulnerabilities detected during runtime.

---

# Step 4.1 - Download Public Datasets

Download the following datasets:

- National Vulnerability Database (NVD)
- Exploit Prediction Scoring System (EPSS)
- CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV)

These datasets serve as the primary training data.

---

# Step 4.2 - Merge the Datasets

Merge all three datasets using the common key:

```
CVE ID
```

Result:

One unified cybersecurity dataset.

Example:

| CVE | CVSS | EPSS | KEV | Attack Vector | ... |

---

# Step 4.3 - Data Preprocessing

Perform preprocessing:

- Remove duplicate records
- Handle missing values
- Encode categorical values
- Select only required features

Final features include:

- CVSS Score
- Attack Vector
- Attack Complexity
- Privileges Required
- User Interaction
- Confidentiality Impact
- Integrity Impact
- Availability Impact
- EPSS Score
- KEV Status

---

# Step 4.4 - Generate Training Labels

Since the public datasets do not directly provide remediation priority labels, labels are generated using a transparent rule-based approach.

Example:

| Condition | Label |
|-----------|-------|
| CVSS ≥ 9 OR KEV = Yes | Critical |
| CVSS 7-8.9 | High |
| CVSS 4-6.9 | Medium |
| Otherwise | Low |

These labels become the target variable for supervised learning.

---

# Step 4.5 - Train the Machine Learning Model

Train the merged dataset using:

- Random Forest (Primary Model)
- XGBoost (Comparison Model)

Both models are evaluated using:

- Accuracy
- Precision
- Recall
- F1-Score

The best-performing model (or a hybrid ensemble if it performs better) becomes the AI Decision Support Engine.

---

# Step 4.6 - Save the Trained Model

Store the trained model so it can be reused during prediction.

The model is **trained only once**.

During runtime, it simply loads the saved model and predicts the priority of new vulnerabilities.

---

# Runtime Prediction Flow

When a user scans a website:

```
Website
        ↓
Assessment Tools
        ↓
Security Findings
        ↓
Identify Product & Version
        ↓
Search NVD
        ↓
Get CVE
        ↓
Query EPSS
        ↓
Query CISA KEV
        ↓
Create Feature Vector
        ↓
Random Forest Model
        ↓
Priority Prediction
        ↓
Gemini (Explainable AI)
        ↓
Dashboard + PDF Report
```

---

# One Important Note (Technically Critical)

This architecture is **technically correct only under one assumption**:

> The security assessment phase can identify enough **specific products/versions or CVEs** to query NVD, EPSS, and CISA KEV.

If your tools only return **generic misconfigurations** (e.g., "Missing CSP", "Missing HSTS"), those findings **cannot** be processed by the ML model because they have no associated CVE. Those findings should follow a **rule-based recommendation path** and later be combined with the ML results in the final report.

With that clarification, this architecture is internally consistent, uses only public datasets for ML training, keeps the training and prediction feature spaces identical, and is realistic for a three-member team to implement within your project timeline.

| Integrity Impact | High |
| Availability Impact | High |
| EPSS Score | 0.93 |

### Step 4.4 - Generate Training Labels

Since the public datasets do not directly provide remediation priority labels, the training labels are generated using a combination of multiple threat intelligence indicators.

The labeling process considers:

- CVSS Severity Score
- EPSS Exploitation Probability
- CISA KEV Status

Example:

| CVSS | EPSS | KEV | Label |
|------|------|------|-------|
| 9.8 | 0.91 | Yes | Critical |
| 8.2 | 0.75 | No | High |
| 6.3 | 0.35 | No | Medium |
| 3.5 | 0.02 | No | Low |

Using multiple indicators instead of only the CVSS score enables the model to learn a more realistic vulnerability prioritization strategy.

# Can we identify Product Name & Version?

## Product Name

**YES** ✅

This is quite feasible.

Using tools like:

- Wappalyzer
- WhatWeb
- Nmap (service detection)
- HTTP response headers

you can often identify technologies such as:

```
Apache
Nginx
WordPress
Tomcat
PHP
Spring Boot
OpenSSL
```

This part is realistic.

---

## Version Number

This is where the problem starts.

Most production websites intentionally hide their version numbers.

Example:

```
Apache
```

is visible.

But

```
Apache 2.4.49
```

is usually hidden.

Many websites remove

```
Server: Apache/2.4.49
```

from headers for security reasons.

So you often get

```
Apache
```

instead of

```
Apache 2.4.49
```

---

# Then how will we know the CVE?

That's exactly the issue.

Without the version,

there may be hundreds of CVEs.

Example

```
Apache

↓

CVE-2017...
CVE-2018...
CVE-2019...
...
CVE-2025...
```

Which one should the ML model use?

You don't know.

---

# Therefore

The architecture I suggested has one assumption:

> **Product version is available.**

In reality,

that assumption is **not always true**.

---

# What do commercial scanners do?

Commercial tools don't magically know every version.

They use:

- Banner grabbing
- Fingerprinting
- Authenticated scans
- Agent-based scanning

Many even require credentials.

You don't have that.

---

# So should we drop the idea?

**Not necessarily.**

But we should **change one thing**.

Instead of saying:

> We map every finding to a CVE.

Say:

> **Whenever a reliable product/version or CVE can be identified, we enrich the finding using NVD, EPSS, and CISA KEV. Otherwise, the finding is handled using rule-based analysis.**

That is technically honest.

---

# Here's the bigger question

After thinking about your constraints again:

- Public datasets only
- 3 months
- Beginners
- Mentor expects ML
- No custom dataset

I actually think we should ask a different question:

> **Can we design the ML model so it does NOT depend on identifying product versions at runtime?**

Because if the answer is yes, the project becomes much more robust.

---

# My recommendation

I think we should spend one session redesigning **only the ML module** with this goal:

- Train on public datasets ✔️
- Use public datasets at runtime ✔️
- Avoid relying on hidden software versions ✔️
- Still satisfy your novelty ✔️

If we achieve that, your project will be much easier to implement and defend.

## I don't want you to spend the next two months building around an assumption that may fail on real websites.

So rather than forcing the architecture to fit, I'd prefer we adjust the ML component now and build something that is both **technically correct and practically reliable**. I think that's the safest path for your project.
